It is off by default because turning it on multiplies everything downstream — message counts, rows, alert thresholds — and a threshold tuned for files that is suddenly counting claims will not mean what it used to. That should be a decision somebody made rather than a default they inherited.

split and acknowledge cannot both be set, and a channel with both is refused at load. An acknowledgement is a statement about a whole interchange, so splitting would send one per transaction set, and a partner receiving several 999s naming the same functional group has no way to reconcile them.

8.3.2 When the counts do not add up

X12 declares its own counts — how many segments, how many transactions — and the envelope key says what to do when they disagree with what is actually present. Those counts are the only mechanism X12 has for detecting that half a claims file arrived.

ValueBehaviour
requireRefuse the interchange. The default.
warnProcess it, and record the fault against the message.
ignoreDo not check at all.

warn exists for a real situation: some partners' software has generated wrong counts for years in otherwise complete files, and a site that has to process those needs a way to say so out loud in the configuration rather than turning validation off wholesale.

ignore is named to be uncomfortable. Setting it throws away the only means X12 gives you of noticing a truncated file, and a truncated claims file is indistinguishable from a small one.

8.3.3 Transactions Perfuse reads as structured data

Any X12 interchange can be routed, filtered and addressed by path. These transactions are additionally parsed into named fields, so a channel can ask what a payer decided rather than which element sits at position four.

TransactionWhat it carries
835Remittance advice: what was paid, what was denied, and the adjustment reasons.
275Claims attachments: clinical documentation supporting a claim.
277Claim status, including a payer's request for additional documentation.
278Prior authorisation: the request for review, and the payer's decision.
999Functional acknowledgement, which Perfuse also generates.
271Eligibility: whether a patient is covered, and their co-payment, coinsurance, deductible and out-of-pocket amounts, in and out of network.
834Enrolment: members added, changed and terminated, with each coverage line and its dates.
837Claims, professional and institutional (read for CMS-0057's CARIN conversion).

Perfuse also builds the 270 eligibility inquiry and the 276 claim status request, under Payers → Claims & auth or through POST /api/x12/eligibility/build and /api/x12/claimstatus/build. Both are the HIPAA 5010 versions (005010X279A1, 005010X212), read back and envelope-checked before they are shown, and neither is sent from that page: delivering one is a channel's job, where the trading partner and its credentials are.

8.3.4 Eligibility, and the CAQH CORE data content rule

A 271 is a list of EB segments, each a single fact: active coverage, or a $25 co-payment for service type 30 in network, or $800 of the individual deductible remaining. The reader assembles them into the answer a front desk wants - "coverage active under Gold PPO 2000; co-payment in network $25; individual deductible remaining in network $800" - and names a rejection (an AAA segment) in words, with what the payer says to do next.

It also checks the response against the CAQH CORE Eligibility & Benefits data content rule: for an inquiry about health benefit plan coverage (service type 30), an active response should carry plan dates, the plan name, co-payment, coinsurance, deductible and remaining deductible both in and out of network, and the base service types (1, 33, 35, 47, 48, 50, 86, 88, 98, AL, MH, UC). Each is listed as met or missing. This is Perfuse's reading of the rule, useful for "why does this payer never tell us the deductible?" - it is not CORE certification, which CAQH's authorised testing vendors carry out.

8.3.5 Prior authorisation

Perfuse implements prior authorisation twice, on purpose.

The FHIR side is what CMS-0057-F requires payers to expose by 1 January 2027. The 278 is what payers accept today, and the rule does not replace it — Da Vinci PAS is in practice a mapping onto this transaction, because a 278 is what a utilisation management system speaks. An engine reading only the FHIR half can talk to the systems that will exist and not to the ones that do.

A 278 response carries the authorisation number, and that is the field the whole transaction exists to deliver. Without it a certified service is not billable: the claim goes in with no authorisation number and is denied for the lack of one, which reads downstream as a clinical denial.

A denial and a refusal to consider are not the same thing

This is the most expensive confusion in the transaction, and Perfuse reports the two separately.

What arrivedWhat it meansWhat to do
HCR with action A3The payer considered the request and will not certify it.Appeal, or change the plan of care.
An AAA segmentThe payer would not consider it — patient not found, requester not recognised, review not covered. Nothing was decided.Correct the request and send it again.

Both read as "not approved" to anybody scanning, and the correct actions are opposite. Appealing something nobody ruled on achieves nothing; resubmitting a denial produces the same denial. The parsed outcome names which happened — denied against not-considered — and AAA04 carries the payer's own advice on whether the request is recoverable at all.

Partial certification is reported as partially-certified rather than folded into approval. There is an authorisation number to bill against, so it counts as approved, but the quantity certified is not the quantity requested — and a practice booking six sessions against three approved discovers it at the fourth claim.

An action code Perfuse does not recognise is reported as unknown, not as pending. Pending reads as "wait", and waiting is the wrong action for most of what an unrecognised code could be.

A channel can route on the decision directly:

filter: HCR-1 == "A3"

Converting between the two

Perfuse converts a parsed 278 into a Da Vinci PAS ClaimResponse, and reports every judgement the conversion needed rather than only the result. The conversion follows three rules:

  • No decision is upgraded. Partial certification stays partial, and a request the payer refused to consider does not become a denial. Both collapses are available in the obvious mapping and both are wrong in the expensive direction.
  • Nothing is invented to fill a required field. A certified event with no authorisation number is reported as such, not given a plausible one — a fabricated number produces a claim that is denied later for a reason nobody can trace.
  • A decision carried only at line level is used. A payer answering per service can leave the event without a decision of its own; reading only the event level would report that as pending, and a practice waits for an answer it already has while the approval expires. Where the lines disagree, the least favourable wins, because an event with a refused line is not an approval.

Narrowing to a three-state vocabulary — approved, denied, pended — is available and explicit about what it discards. Narrowing a refusal to consider yields "denied" and says in words that the correct action is a corrected resubmission rather than an appeal.

Checked against PAS 2.2.1, not only against itself

The ClaimResponse is shaped to the Da Vinci PAS 2.2.1 profile and was checked with the official HL7 validator loaded with the published PAS package. The first version had only been checked by Perfuse's own tests, and the validator rejected it with six to eight errors per resource. Every one of them is something a payer's PAS endpoint would also have rejected:

What was wrongWhere PAS puts it
The decision was in adjudication.category and reason.The reviewAction extension, on an adjudication whose category is submitted.
The reason code named X12 element 1034.X12 external code list 886.
A pended request answered outcome: queued.outcome is about processing and PAS allows only complete, error and partial. A pend is complete, with review action A4.
processNote.type was a CodeableConcept.R4 has a code: print.
patient, created, insurer and request were missing.All four are required. They are given as identifier references, because the response stands alone rather than in a bundle.

The corrected output validates with no errors for a certification, a denial and a pend. The warnings that remain are the validator saying it cannot load X12's code systems, which X12 licenses. The authorisation number is carried in reviewAction.number, where the guide's own example puts it, and in preAuthRef when the response has only one.

The validator is not part of make check, because it needs Java and a download of the guide and its dependencies. What it found is held by a test instead: each rule it enforced is asserted, so a change that reintroduces one of those errors fails.

The Claims & auth view converts a pasted 278 and shows the decisions, the notes and the resource.

8.3.6 Attachments, and why they need special handling

CMS-0053-F makes the 275 a HIPAA standard for claims attachments, with compliance required by 26 May 2028. It carries a document — an operative note, an imaging report, a scanned form — tied to the claim it supports.

The 277 is the other half of the same conversation. A payer receives a claim, decides it needs documentation, and sends a 277 asking for it; the provider answers with a 275.

A channel can route on the status code directly, because STC01 is a composite and its second component is the status. Written in canonical form, because a component is addressed with a dot and the two notations cannot be mixed — STC-1.2 is element 1, component 2:

filter: STC-1.2 in ("227", "233", "252", "287")

Those are the codes that unambiguously mean a document is wanted. The status category in STC-1.1 is deliberately not used on its own: R4 means a documentation request in some implementation guides and "forwarded elsewhere" in others, so a filter on the category alone would put finalised claims in the queue for somebody to chase.

Two identifiers decide whether an attachment is ever associated with its claim, and both are reported: the attachment control number, which the provider put on the original claim to say documentation would follow, and the trace number, which matches the payer's request. An attachment arriving with neither is filed and never connected to anything, while the claim ages.

A document inside a 275 is not interpreted. Perfuse reports its bytes and what the sender says they are. An attachment may be a PDF, a TIFF, a CDA document or an HL7 message, and a channel needing the content can hand those bytes to the format that reads it.

The 006020 form, which is the one the rule adopts

CMS-0053-F adopts version 006020, and its 275 differs from the 005010 form in two ways that matter.

  • The payload is in BDS, not BIN. BDS01 names a filter, ASC or B64. BDS02 is the length and BDS03 the data.
  • The payload is a MIME entity, with headers saying what the document is and a base64 body. With B64 the entity itself is base64 as well.

Perfuse unwraps the entity and reports the document inside it, with its content type and file name. The bytes it reports are exactly the ones the sender wrapped. An entity that cannot be unwrapped leaves the raw payload in place, with a note saying why.

Building a 275. Perfuse also builds 006020X314 attachments: an unsolicited one to accompany a claim, or a response to a payer's 277 request. The difference is BGN01 02 against 11, and whether TRN02 carries the claim's PWK06 or the 277's TRN02. Every built interchange is parsed back before it is returned, and the response says whether the document inside is the one that went in.

The structure comes from the two published companion guides for 006020X314: the CMS esMD guide and UnitedHealthcare's. It does not come from the X12 technical report, which X12 licenses and which this project does not hold.

  • Where the two guides agree, Perfuse follows them.
  • Where they differ, it satisfies both if it can. CAT02 is HL for an HL7 document, which esMD uses, and IA for an image, which UnitedHealthcare uses.
  • Otherwise it sends the optional segment only when given the value: esMD's REF*X1 claim identifier and UnitedHealthcare's DTP*472 service date.

There is no validation against the technical report. The partner's companion guide governs.

8.3.7 Electronic signatures on attachments

CMS-0053-F also adopts a signature standard, at 45 CFR 162.2002(e): the HL7 Implementation Guide for CDA Release 2, Digital Signatures and Delegation of Rights, Release 1. It applies to signatures put on attachment information when it is sent in a HIPAA attachment transaction, from 26 May 2028. A payer may not ask for more than the guide does.

Perfuse signs a C-CDA that way on its way into a 275, and checks the signatures on every C-CDA in a 275 it reads.

  • What is signed. The whole document except its legalAuthenticator and authenticator participants, so each later signer leaves the earlier signatures intact. The signature says so with an XPath Filter 2.0 transform any XML signature library can evaluate.
  • Where it goes. Base64 in sdtc:signatureText on the signing participant, with a one-line plain-text description. The signer is the document's legal authenticator if it has an unsigned one, otherwise a new authenticator.
  • What it states. The signer's certificate, the time in UTC, a role from the NUCC provider taxonomy and a purpose from ASTM E1762. The purpose defaults to *Author's signature* for a legal authenticator and *Coauthor's signature* otherwise, as in the guide's own example.
  • How long it lasts. The guide asks for XAdES-X-L, which carries everything a payer needs to verify the signature years later without reaching the network. That is the certification path, an OCSP response or CRL from the CA, and two time-stamps from a time-stamping authority. Perfuse fetches the revocation data from the addresses in the certificate and the time-stamps from -tsa-url.

If any of that is unavailable, the signature is still made and holds. The result names the XAdES form it reached, and says what stopped it short of X-L: for example a self-signed certificate (no CA to ask) or no time-stamping authority.

The key is -signing-cert and -signing-key. Put the CA certificates after the signer's in the same file so the path travels with the signature. For a signature that means what DSDR means, the certificate should name the clinician, with their NPI, rather than the server. Signing is an admin action, as signing any document is.

The guide does not name a namespace for its own elements. Perfuse puts digitalSignature, authorizedSigner and SignaturePurpose in urn:hl7-org:sdtc. It also states the purpose as a XAdES commitment type with the ASTM OID, which XAdES tools understand. Delegation of rights, where a delegate signs with a SAML assertion from the authorised signer, is not produced, and a received one is reported as not verified.

The provider NPI is checked against its check digit. A payer matches an attachment to its claim partly on that number, and a mistyped one leaves the attachment unmatched while the claim is denied for missing documentation.

The Claims & auth view builds a 275 from a pasted C-CDA or an uploaded file, optionally signing the C-CDA, and reads one back with each signature's findings. Nothing on that page sends anything: the interchange is handed back to be delivered through a channel, which is where the partner, the credentials and the audit trail belong.

8.3.8 Binary segments

A BIN segment carries raw bytes, and raw bytes contain delimiters. Every other X12 segment ends at the segment terminator, so an ordinary parser finds segments by searching for one byte; an attachment cannot be found that way. A PDF containing a ~ would be cut into pieces, and each piece is a structurally valid segment — so the document is truncated, every segment after it is misaligned, and nothing reports an error.

Perfuse reads the byte count the segment declares and takes exactly that many bytes. Two consequences worth knowing:

  • A declared length that does not match the data is refused, naming both numbers. The useful question is whether the sender's count was wrong or the file was truncated in transit, and only the two numbers answer it.
  • BDS, the 006020 binary segment, keeps its count in BDS02 and its data in BDS03. It was refused until that could be confirmed from a published source rather than recalled, and two sources now confirm it: the CMS esMD companion guide's BDS table and UnitedHealthcare's guide.

The esMD guide's own sample interchange declares 16 bytes for the same 53-byte payload that its table correctly counts as 53. Perfuse refuses that sample, as it should. A test holds both the table's example and the sample's typo, so a change in either direction is noticed.

8.4 FHIR

8.4.1 What an HL7 v2 message becomes

The message type decides what is produced, and every conversion reports the judgements it needed as notes beside the bundle.

v2FHIR
ADTPatient, an Encounter when there is a visit, a Condition per DG1 diagnosis and an AllergyIntolerance per AL1. A28, A31, A29 and A24 are about the person, so they produce no Encounter or diagnoses, but do carry allergies.
ORUDiagnosticReport per OBR, with its Observations and Specimen. Results are grouped under the OBR before them by position, because OBX-4 is not reliable.
ORM, OML, OMGServiceRequest.
SIUAppointment, with the patient, the practitioners from AIP and the locations from AIL.
MDMDocumentReference carrying the document.
VXUImmunization, one per RXA.

Anything else produces the Patient and a warning naming the type that was not mapped.

Scheduling. A booking, a reschedule, a cancellation and a no-show all land on the same Appointment. Its identity comes from the appointment's own numbers in SCH-1 and SCH-2, never from the message, so a cancellation updates the booking. The alternative would be a second, cancelled appointment beside a live one, which shows the patient booked into a slot that no longer exists.

  • Status. The filler status in SCH-25 decides it, and the trigger event is used only when that is empty.
  • Blocked slots. A blocked slot is not an appointment with a patient, and produces none.
  • Start time. It is read from AIS, AIG, AIL or AIP first, then SCH-11, because version 2.5 moved it and feeds did not all move with it.
  • Durations. A duration in a unit Perfuse does not recognise is left out, not guessed.

Documents. Text notes (TX, FT, ST) keep their line breaks, including blank lines, because a blank repetition in a note is a paragraph break.

  • Embedded documents. An embedded PDF, TIFF or JPEG (ED) is carried byte for byte. Base64 that is not valid base64 is refused rather than passed on.
  • Types. The document type in TXA-2 is kept as sent and, where there is a clear equivalent, given the LOINC code US Core uses.
  • Signing. A note nobody has signed is preliminary, and T11 makes the document entered-in-error.
  • No content. A notification with no content (T01, T03 and the other odd-numbered events) produces no DocumentReference. FHIR requires content, and an empty attachment would look like the note while saying nothing.

This is what CMS Interoperability Framework criterion 14 asks for: chart notes and clinical documents returned as FHIR attachments.

Immunizations. A dose is never reported as given when the message says otherwise.

  • Refused doses. A refused or not-administered dose (RXA-20 RE or NA) is not-done, with the reason from RXA-18.
  • Deletions. A deleted one (RXA-21 D) is entered-in-error.
  • Historical records. A record transcribed from somewhere else (RXA-9 other than 00) is not a primary source.
  • Unknown amounts. An amount of 999 means unknown, not 999.
  • Routes and sites. Each dose takes the route and site from the RXR after it, so a message with two doses does not give the second one the first one's route.

Awkward input. Each of these is reported in the notes rather than decided silently:

  • Identifiers. PID-2 and PID-4, withdrawn after v2.7 but still sent, are kept like PID-3. CX.7 and CX.8 become

Identifier.period. An assigning authority carrying an ISO OID (AUSHIC&2.16.840.1.113883.3.879&ISO) becomes urn:oid:. A type code outside HL7 table 0203 is kept as text, not labelled as one.

  • Merges. MRG on a merge event (A40 and its kin) sends the retired record too, inactive, with Patient.link both ways. On any

other event, such as an A08, nothing is merged and a warning says the prior identifier is still live.

  • Times. A time of birth in PID-7 goes to the patient-birthTime extension. A timestamp with no offset takes the configured

timezone, otherwise the offset on MSH-7 (v2 presumes the sender's local time), otherwise UTC. A time of birth is the exception: with no offset from any of the three it is left out rather than labelled UTC, because a wrong offset can move a birth to another day. birthDate is kept, and a warning says the time was dropped and that setting a timezone for the feed keeps it.

  • Results. Repeats of a text result are joined with line breaks. An ED result, or a coded one whose value is plainly Base64, becomes a

DocumentReference that the Observation points to in derivedFrom, since an R4 Observation has no attachment value. An SN comparator of = is dropped as redundant; <> keeps the text, because no quantity can say "not equal".

  • Visits. An A08 with a discharge date (PV1-45) is a finished encounter. A missing or unknown patient class is the null

flavor UNK, because R4 requires a class.

  • Z segments are named in a warning, since there is no standard map for them.

8.4.2 US Core 9.0.0 and USCDI v6

-us-core (and claim_us_core on a fhir destination) puts a US Core profile in meta.profile, but only on a resource that has been checked against that profile and found to conform. One that falls short is still converted and sent without the claim, and a note gives the reason, such as "US Core requires an encounter type, and this Encounter has none". The checks are transcribed from US Core 9.0.0, the version ONC's 2026 Standards Version Advancement Process approves for USCDI v6. They cover Patient, Encounter, the laboratory Observation and DiagnosticReport, Specimen, Practitioner, Organization, Location, Immunization, DocumentReference, the encounter-diagnosis Condition and AllergyIntolerance.

What the converter carries, by USCDI v6 data class:

USCDI v6FromAs
Patient DemographicsPIDName, birth date, sex, address, phone and email, identifiers. Race and ethnicity (PID-10, PID-22) in US Core's extensions when coded in CDC Race and Ethnicity. Preferred language (PID-15) as BCP 47
Encounter InformationPV1Encounter with type, class, period, location and participants
Facility InformationPV1-3Location, with the facility as its managing Organization
Problems, Date of OnsetDG1Condition (encounter diagnosis). DG1-5 is the onset, as the HL7 v2-to-FHIR map has it
Allergies and Intolerances, ReactionAL1AllergyIntolerance with category, criticality and reaction
LaboratoryORUDiagnosticReport, Observations, Specimen (from SPM)
OrdersORM, OMLServiceRequest
ImmunizationsVXUImmunization
Clinical NotesMDMDocumentReference
Care Team MembersPV1, TXA, DG1Practitioner

Not carried, because v2 messages of these types do not hold them or Perfuse does not map them: Medications, Procedures, Goals and Preferences, Health Status Assessments, Care Plan, Family Health History, Medical Devices (UDI), Health Insurance Information, Vital Signs as a distinct profile, and Provenance.

8.4.3 R4 in both directions

A FHIR server declaring R4 accepts R4. That was not true of Encounters until this release. The server converted what it served to R4 but read what it received as if it were already in its internal form, so an R4 Encounter was refused with a 400. So was every bundle Perfuse's own ADT conversion produced, because each carries Encounter.class, which R4 writes as a single coding.

It was found the first time anything posted a converted ADT to Perfuse's own R4 server. A test now does that for every mapped message type, and another checks that everything written as R4 is read back unchanged.

8.4.4 International Patient Summary

GET /fhir/Patient/{id}/$summary, or GET /fhir/Patient/$summary?identifier=system|value, returns an International Patient Summary (HL7 IPS 2.0.0): a FHIR document Bundle whose Composition lists the patient's active problems, allergies, medicines, immunizations, results, vital signs, procedures, devices, past illnesses, social history and care plans, each with a readable narrative. It is the short record a clinician who has never met the patient reads first, and the summary the European Health Data Space exchanges between countries from 2029.

It is built from what the FHIR store holds, and nothing is invented:

  • Records marked entered-in-error are left out.
  • A resolved condition goes under past illness, not problems.
  • A medicine order that was stopped or completed is history, not a current medicine.
  • The three sections the IPS requires (problems, allergies, medicines) are always present. When one has nothing in it, the section

carries the coded empty reason unavailable and says the server holds no information, "which does not mean there is none". An empty allergy list must never read as "no known allergies".

  • The practitioners, organisations, medicines and locations the entries point at are included, so the document reads on its own.

Access follows $everything: a token limited to one patient gets a 403 for another, and an identifier that matches two patients is refused rather than guessed. Only the IPS profile is built; asking for another profile is refused. The document Perfuse produces from its test records validates against the IPS 2.0.0 Bundle profile with the HL7 validator, with no errors. A record built from real feeds can still fail where the feed's own codes do (a display name the code system does not use, for example), because the summary passes the data on as it was received.

Exchange → Health links → Share fills the content with a patient's summary: type the patient's FHIR id and choose Use their patient summary, then read it and create the link. Building it is audited as fhir.summary.

8.4.5 Subscriptions

With -fhir-subscriptions, the FHIR endpoint sends notifications when an Encounter or an Appointment is created or changed. It follows the FHIR Subscriptions R5 Backport IG for R4, which is what CMS Interoperability Framework criterion 15 and TEFCA's FHIR roadmap use for appointment and encounter notifications.

A client creates a Subscription naming a topic, and optionally a filter such as Encounter?patient=Patient/123. The topics are listed in the capability statement:

TopicFires onFilters
https://perfuse.health/fhir/SubscriptionTopic/encounterEncounter created or updatedpatient, status, class
https://perfuse.health/fhir/SubscriptionTopic/appointmentAppointment created or updatedpatient, status

A filter that names anything else is refused, not ignored. An ignored filter is a subscription that notifies about every patient when the client asked about one.

The properties the implementation is built around:

  • An acknowledged change is never a lost notification. The notification is recorded in the same database transaction as the change it describes, and delivered from there, so it survives a restart. A test writes an Encounter, starts a new server on the same database, and checks the notification arrives.
  • Notifications arrive in order, and gaps are visible. Event 2 is not sent before event 1 is accepted, and each notification carries the running count, so a receiver can tell when it has missed one.
  • The server decides when a subscription is active. A new subscription is requested, and becomes active only when the endpoint accepts a handshake. A client that asks for active gets requested. A refused handshake is tried again four times over about fifteen seconds before the subscription is an error: it goes out the moment the subscription is stored, often before the client that created it is listening.
  • A full-resource notification carries the version that changed. A discharge notification carries the discharge, not whatever the Encounter had become by the time delivery succeeded.
  • Failure is bounded. A refused delivery is retried with a growing delay. After ten consecutive failures the subscription goes to error: later changes are counted but not queued, and updating the subscription resumes delivery from the oldest held notification. The count then shows how many were missed.

What a client can make the server do is limited deliberately:

  • Endpoints must be https unless the operator passes -fhir-subscriptions-allow-http, which is for a test receiver.
  • The egress policy is applied when a subscription is created, again at every delivery, and on the address each connection actually reaches (so a name that changes what it resolves to between the check and the connection is still refused); redirects are not followed.
  • The payload defaults to id-only, which says what changed and lets the receiver fetch it with its own credentials.
  • Nothing a receiver sends back is shown to anybody, only its status code.

It is off unless asked for, because once on, a FHIR client can make this server send patient data to a URL of the client's choosing. It needs an R4 endpoint that accepts writes, and refuses to start otherwise.

Not implemented, so nobody has to find out by trying:

  • channels other than rest-hook
  • heartbeats
  • notifications on delete
  • the $status and $events operations

The Subscriptions view shows each subscription's topic, filter and endpoint, how many events it has had, how many were accepted and how many are waiting, and the last reason one was refused. Header values and the endpoint's query string are withheld, because that is where a subscriber's credentials live.

8.5 NCPDP Telecommunication Standard

Pharmacy claims. Fixed-width header followed by variable segments.

Two details that matter, both of which are easy to get wrong and produce plausible results:

The header is exactly 56 bytes and fixed width. A short header cannot be detected by length alone, because a truncated header plus the following segments still exceeds 56 bytes. Detection is that a correct header never contains a separator byte, so the header is scanned for one and a message that has one is refused with the number of bytes it is short by.

0x1E and 0x1C are group and field start markers, not separators. Treating them as separators shifts every field one place left, and most of the resulting values stay plausible — a quantity lands where a day supply was expected and both are numbers.

NCPDP and SCRIPT channels are excluded from MLLP framing, and this is enforced rather than advised. A claim legitimately contains 0x1C, which is MLLP's own end-of-block byte, so framing a claim would truncate it at the first field marker.

8.6 NCPDP SCRIPT

Electronic prescriptions, in XML. The internal package is called eprescribe because script was taken by the JavaScript engine.

The central safety concern is the substitution flag, and it reads backwards from the intuition: on the wire, 0 means substitution is allowed and 1 means dispense as written.

An absent substitution flag is refused rather than defaulted. Both possible defaults reach the patient — one may dispense a generic where the prescriber required the brand, the other prevents a substitution the prescriber permitted — so there is no safe assumption and the message stops.

A Schedule II prescription with refills is refused, and the builder will not produce one.

Refusals and warnings are kept as separate lists rather than one severity-tagged list, because a reader who has to check a severity field to know whether the message was sent will eventually not check it.

Electronic prior authorization (PAInitiationRequest, PARequest, PAAppealRequest, PACancelRequest) and Real-Time Prescription Benefit are answered by the payer side at /ncpdp/script and /ncpdp/rtpb, from the drug formulary; see CMS-0057.

8.7 DICOM

Imaging. Perfuse can receive DICOM objects, query a remote archive, and store to one, over the classic DICOM network services or over DICOMweb, and it can keep an archive of its own for viewers.

DICOM is not a message format in the same sense as the others — objects are large, and the association handshake matters more than the payload parsing. The called_ae and calling_ae titles are the commonest reason a first connection fails, and the failure looks like a network problem rather than a configuration one.

8.7.1 What a C-STORE listener accepts

Left open, a DICOM listener accepts anything from anyone that can reach the port. Four settings under What this listener accepts narrow that, and the first is the one worth understanding.

Only accept these calling AE titles is the restriction. The *called* AE title — the one alongside the address — is the name a sender dials, so it identifies this listener rather than the sender, and it is not a restriction. Anybody who can reach the port can use the correct called AE title, because it is published to them. Without a list of permitted callers, any host on the network may push images into a clinical channel.

Only accept these SOP classes and only accept these transfer syntaxes take comma-separated UIDs. Empty accepts every kind of object and negotiates whatever compression the sender offers, which includes kinds this channel's destinations cannot handle. Refusing at the association is clearer to the sender than accepting the object and failing afterwards.

Largest object accepted is in bytes, and zero means no limit. Whole-slide images and long series run to gigabytes, so the limit is otherwise memory.

8.7.2 DICOMweb

DICOMweb is DICOM over HTTP: QIDO-RS searches, WADO-RS and WADO-URI retrieve, STOW-RS stores. Browser viewers such as OHIF speak only DICOMweb, and so do the cloud imaging services (Google Cloud Healthcare, Azure Health Data Services, AWS HealthImaging). Perfuse uses it in three places.

The imaging archive. Start the server with -dicomweb-archive DIR (Settings › Data › Imaging archive) and it keeps objects in that folder, indexed in SQLite, and serves them at /dicomweb on the console's own address: QIDO-RS at the study, series and instance levels, WADO-RS for objects, metadata, frames and bulk data, WADO-URI at /dicomweb/wado, and STOW-RS. Reading takes the viewer role and storing the operator role, with a session or an API token; every read and store is in Activity as dicomweb.read or dicomweb.store. An account limited to a tenant or to channel groups is refused, because the archive belongs to the whole server. Objects are kept exactly as received, so what a viewer retrieves is byte for byte what was stored. Frames are returned in the transfer syntax they were stored in; a request that would need transcoding answers 406 rather than a converted image.

A search on an attribute the archive does not index answers 400 rather than ignoring the condition and returning more studies than were asked for. Indexed: patient name (wildcards, and fuzzy matching with fuzzymatching=true), patient ID, birth date, sex, study date and time (ranges), accession number, study ID, description, referring physician, study, series and SOP instance UIDs (lists), modality, body part, series number and description, SOP class, instance number, rows, columns and number of frames.

The source. type: dicomweb listens for STOW-RS, at /dicom-web/studies by default (the path Orthanc and dcm4chee use), and sends every object through the channel as a DICOM message. The channel needs dataType: dicom. Set a bearer token or a username and password: without one, anybody who can reach the port can send images into the channel. The answer lists each object as stored or failed, with the DICOM failure reason, so a sender knows which to resend.

name: imaging-from-viewers
dataType: dicom
source:
  type: dicomweb
  dicomweb:
    listen: 0.0.0.0:8043
    token: ${STOW_TOKEN}
    tls: { enabled: true, cert_file: /etc/perfuse/imaging.crt, key_file: /etc/perfuse/imaging.key }
destinations:
  - name: archive
    type: dicomweb
    dicomweb:
      url: https://perfuse.example/dicomweb
      token: ${ARCHIVE_TOKEN}

The destination is in delivery, under DICOMweb archives.

Pointing OHIF at the archive

Use a DICOMweb data source with qidoRoot and wadoRoot set to the archive's address (the Imaging screen shows it, with the whole data-source block to copy), wadoUriRoot to that address followed by /wado, imageRendering and thumbnailRendering to wadors, and supportsFuzzyMatching true. A viewer on another origin needs that origin in -dicomweb-cors (Browser origins allowed to call it), and its requests need an API token in the Authorization header, because a browser on another origin does not send the console's session cookie. Serving OHIF from the same origin through a reverse proxy avoids both.

Tested against Orthanc with its DICOMweb plugin, in both directions: the destination stores into Orthanc, and Orthanc's DICOMweb client searches Perfuse's archive and retrieves a study byte-identical to the one stored. OHIF 3 lists the archive's studies and displays their frames with the settings above. OHIF and several other clients send type= unquoted in the Accept and Content-Type headers, which RFC 2045 does not allow; Perfuse accepts it.

8.8 IHE document sharing: XDS.b, XCA, MHD, PIXm and PDQm

Clinical documents shared between organisations the way health information exchanges, Carequality and eHealth Exchange do: a repository holds them, a registry indexes their metadata, and a community answers other communities' queries through a gateway. Start the server with -xds REPOSITORY-OID (Settings › Data › Document sharing) and it is all three, in this server's database, at these addresses, each taking an API token as a bearer token (the viewer role to query and retrieve, the operator role to submit; most affinity domains add mutual TLS in front):

AddressActorTransactions
/xds/repositoryDocument RepositoryITI-41 Provide and Register Document Set-b, ITI-43 Retrieve Document Set
/xds/registryDocument RegistryITI-18 Registry Stored Query (all thirteen stored queries), ITI-42 Register Document Set-b
/xca/gatewayXCA Responding GatewayITI-38 Cross Gateway Query, ITI-39 Cross Gateway Retrieve, with -xds-home-community urn:oid:...
/mhdMHD Document Recipient and ResponderITI-65 Provide Document Bundle, ITI-66 Find Lists, ITI-67 Find Document References, ITI-68 Retrieve Document
/mhd/PatientPDQm Supplier and PIXm ManagerITI-78 Patient Demographics Query, ITI-83 Patient/$ihe-pix, from the FHIR store's Patients

The SOAP transactions are SOAP 1.2 with WS-Addressing, and MTOM/XOP where documents travel (a document inline as base64 is accepted too). A failed transaction is answered with a RegistryResponse carrying IHE's error codes, not a SOAP fault: XDSDuplicateUniqueIdInRegistry, XDSNonIdenticalHash, XDSPatientIdDoesNotMatch, XDSMissingDocument, XDSRegistryMetadataError (with the field that is missing), XDSStoredQueryMissingParam, XDSUnknownStoredQuery, XDSUnknownRepositoryId, XDSUnknownCommunity, and UnresolvedReferenceException. A replacement (RPLC, XFRM_RPLC) deprecates the document it replaces, which then answers only queries asking for deprecated documents; APND, XFRM and signs are kept as associations. Hash and size are computed by the repository and checked against what the submission says. On-demand documents and metadata update (ITI-57) are not supported, and a submission asking for them is refused with the reason.

MHD is the same registry in FHIR: a document provided over MHD is found by ITI-18 and over XCA, and one provided over XDS.b is found by ITI-67. A DocumentReference's id is the entry's UUID, its masterIdentifier the uniqueId, and its attachment's url the ITI-68 address of the document. Patients are resolved to the affinity domain's identifier from the FHIR store: the first identifier whose system is urn:oid:, or the Patient carried in the Bundle. PIXm answers every other identifier of the Patient with the one given (sourceIdentifier=system|value, narrowed by targetSystem), and 404 for one it does not know; PDQm searches by identifier, name, birth date, gender, address and telecom.

Publishing. A channel's xds destination publishes each clinical document it receives, a CDA document or one carried in an HL7 v2 message, to any XDS.b repository with ITI-41. The metadata is read from the document: the patient (the identifier in patient_domain, the affinity domain's assigning authority), title, type (as typeCode and, unless set, classCode), creation and service times, author, confidentiality, language and format (C-CDA 2.1 or 1.1 structured body, XDS-SD, or mimeTypeSufficient, from its templates); and completed from what a document does not say: the sender's source_id, the facility_type and practice_setting every registry requires. The repository's RegistryResponse decides the delivery: a Failure, or an error in a PartialSuccess, is a failed delivery with the registry's error codes.

destinations:
  - name: hie
    type: xds
    xds:
      url: https://hie.example/xds/repository
      source_id: 1.2.840.114350.1.13.99
      patient_domain: 1.2.840.114350.1.13.99.2.7.5.737384.14
      facility_type: { code: "225732001", scheme: 2.16.840.1.113883.6.96, display: Community hospital }
      practice_setting: { code: "394802001", scheme: 2.16.840.1.113883.6.96, display: General medicine }
      tls: { enabled: true, cert_file: /etc/perfuse/hie.crt, key_file: /etc/perfuse/hie.key }

Exchange › Document sharing shows what the registry holds and where it is reached, finds a patient's documents in this registry or another (a registry's ITI-18 or a gateway's ITI-38), downloads one, and publishes a CDA document by hand with the metadata a destination would give it.

Verified against the NIST XDS Toolkit 7.13.0, the IHE reference implementation: Perfuse's ITI-41, ITI-18 and ITI-43 against its Registry/Repository simulator (accepted with no validation errors, the document retrieved byte-identical), and its Initiating Gateway simulator querying and retrieving from Perfuse's Responding Gateway over XCA, with no errors in its log. Every SOAP message Perfuse writes validates against the ebXML Registry 3.0 and XDS.b schemas, and the MHD DocumentReference and SubmissionSet against IHE MHD 4.2.2 with the HL7 FHIR validator. Not yet checked against Gazelle or at a Connectathon, and PIX/PDQ V3 (ITI-44, ITI-45, ITI-47 over SOAP) is not built.

8.9 Direct secure messaging

Direct is how US providers send each other referrals, summaries and results by secure e-mail: every message is signed with the sender's certificate and encrypted to the recipient's (S/MIME), and travels between HISPs (Health Information Service Providers) that issue the addresses and vouch for them. Perfuse is the edge system beside your HISP, not a HISP: the HISP issues your address, its certificate and its key, and you keep its trust bundle (such as DirectTrust's). It follows the Direct Applicability Statement for Secure Health Transport 1.2, the Implementation Guide for Delivery Notification, and IHE XDM (ITI TF-1 Annex 15) for documents with metadata.

Sending. A direct destination signs each message as your address (SHA-256, RSA), encrypts it to every recipient's certificate and your own (AES-256-CBC, RSA key transport), and submits it to the HISP by SMTP with STARTTLS required. The message travels as an attachment named by its content (document.xml, message.hl7, resource.json), or, with xdm: true, a CDA document travels as an IHE XDM package (IHE_XDM/SUBSET01/METADATA.XML with the document and its hash and size, plus INDEX.HTM and README.TXT) that receiving EHRs import with its metadata. Recipient certificates come from the Direct address book (below), then from DNS, where Direct publishes them as CERT records (RFC 4398): under the address with its @ made a dot, else under its domain. A certificate is used only when it is for the address or its domain and chains to one of your anchors; a recipient with no such certificate is a failed delivery, never plain e-mail. to and subject take field references such as {PV1-8.1}, and request_mdn asks the recipient for a delivery notification.

destinations:
  - name: referrals
    type: direct
    direct:
      address: referrals@direct.hospital.example
      cert_file: /etc/perfuse/direct/referrals.crt   # with its chain after it
      key_file: /etc/perfuse/direct/referrals.key
      anchors_file: /etc/perfuse/direct/anchors.pem
      to: ["intake@direct.clinic.example"]
      subject: "Referral for {PID-5.1}"
      text: "Please see the attached summary."
      xdm: true
      xdm_source_id: 1.2.840.114350.1.13.99
      smtp_host: smtp.hisp.example:587
      username: referrals
      password: ${DIRECT_HISP_PASSWORD}
      request_mdn: true

Receiving. A direct source is the SMTP server the HISP delivers your address's mail to. It takes mail for that address only (anything else is refused at RCPT), requires STARTTLS when given a certificate, and opens each message before anything reads it: decrypts it with your key, verifies the signature against your anchors, and checks that the signing certificate is for the From address or its domain. A message that is not encrypted S/MIME, is signed by a sender you do not trust, or is signed by a certificate for someone else is refused at DATA with the reason, so the HISP keeps it and reports it to the sender; Perfuse never accepts what it then drops. An XDM package's documents each become a message (checked against the hashes in its metadata); otherwise each attachment does, or the text when there is none. With smtp_host set, each message taken in is answered with a signed, encrypted "processed" notification (MDN), and notifications arriving for what you sent are logged.

source:
  type: direct
  direct:
    address: intake@direct.clinic.example
    cert_file: /etc/perfuse/direct/intake.crt
    key_file: /etc/perfuse/direct/intake.key
    anchors_file: /etc/perfuse/direct/anchors.pem
    listen: 0.0.0.0:2525
    tls_cert: /etc/perfuse/tls/server.crt
    tls_key: /etc/perfuse/tls/server.key
    smtp_host: smtp.hisp.example:587

Exchange › Direct lists the Direct addresses your channels send and receive as, with their certificates, expiry and anchors; keeps the address book of correspondents (name, organisation, notes and certificate, pasted from their HISP or found in DNS, and refused when it is not for that address or domain); and looks up an address's certificates in DNS without saving them. The book is the directory direct-certs beside the database, one PEM file per address or domain, which a destination reads unless it names its own recipient_certs_dir.

Verified against OpenSSL 4.0's cms and smime in both directions: what Perfuse signs and encrypts OpenSSL verifies and decrypts (PKCS#1 v1.5 and OAEP key transport), and Perfuse opens what OpenSSL signs and encrypts (AES-128 and AES-256). A message made with openssl smime and delivered over SMTP is received, opened and stored by a channel, and one Perfuse sends a message to another through a HISP stand-in, which answers with an MDN the sender opens. Not yet tested against an accredited HISP or the ONC Edge Testing Tool; IMAP collection from a HISP mailbox, XDR (Direct over SOAP), and LDAP certificate discovery are not built.

8.10 SQL on FHIR views

A ViewDefinition (HL7 SQL on FHIR v2) turns each FHIR resource of one type into rows of named columns, each column a FHIRPath expression, so FHIR data can be read as tables by DuckDB, Athena, Spark, a data warehouse or a spreadsheet. Perfuse runs them with its own FHIRPath engine: column, nested select, forEach, forEachOrNull, repeat and unionAll, where, constant (every value[x] type), collection columns, %rowIndex, and the getResourceKey() and getReferenceKey(type) join keys.

{
  "resourceType": "ViewDefinition",
  "name": "blood_pressure",
  "resource": "Observation",
  "constant": [{ "name": "systolic", "valueCode": "8480-6" }],
  "where": [{ "path": "code.coding.exists(system = 'http://loinc.org' and code = '85354-9')" }],
  "select": [{ "column": [
    { "name": "id", "path": "getResourceKey()", "type": "id" },
    { "name": "patient_id", "path": "subject.getReferenceKey(Patient)", "type": "id" },
    { "name": "systolic", "path": "component.where(code.coding.code = %systolic).value.ofType(Quantity).value.first()", "type": "decimal" }
  ]}]
}

Monitor › SQL on FHIR runs a view over this server's FHIR store (one patient, or all) or over resources pasted in (NDJSON from a bulk export, a Bundle, a JSON array), shows the rows, and downloads them as CSV, NDJSON or Parquet. It starts from five example views (patient demographics, observations with a value, blood pressure, conditions, encounters) and saves views to the views directory beside the database, one JSON file each.

The FHIR endpoint answers the specification's $sql-run at the system level, by GET or POST, for a ViewDefinition subject given inline (subjectResource), by subjectReference (ViewDefinition/<name>, a saved view) or by subjectCanonical (a saved view's url), with _format (csv, ndjson (the default), json or parquet), header, patient, _since, _limit, and resource to run over resources sent with the request instead of the store. It is a read: a SMART token needs read scope for the view's resource type, a token bound to one patient gets that patient's rows only, and granular scopes narrow the resources read, as they do a search. More than a million rows needs _limit, patient or _since.

perfuse sof -view v.json [-format csv|ndjson|json|parquet] [-o out] files... runs a view over FHIR files without a server: the NDJSON files of a bulk export, Bundles or arrays.

perfuse sof -view blood_pressure.json -format parquet -o bp.parquet export/Observation.ndjson
duckdb -c "SELECT patient_id, avg(systolic) FROM 'bp.parquet' GROUP BY 1"

Parquet files have one row group and uncompressed PLAIN pages; booleans are BOOLEAN, integer types INT64, decimals DOUBLE and everything else UTF-8 strings (a collection column as its JSON).

Verified with the official SQL on FHIR test suite (the 144 tests of the sql-on-fhir.js repository's tests/, run on every build: all pass), and the Parquet output read back by DuckDB 1.5.6 with the right types and values. Not supported: SQLQuery and SQLView Libraries (SQL over the views' tables), $sql-export, _format=fhir, and the source and group parameters; each is refused with the reason.

8.11 Digital quality measures

A digital quality measure counts the patients in each population of a FHIR Measure over a measurement period (initial population, denominator, denominator exclusions and exceptions, numerator, numerator exclusions) and scores the result. The answer is a FHIR MeasureReport, shaped as the Da Vinci Data Exchange for Quality Measures (DEQM) guide asks. This is where CMS's eCQM programmes and NCQA's digital HEDIS are heading.

In Perfuse the population criteria are FHIRPath ("language": "text/fhirpath"), run by the same engine as SQL on FHIR. Each criterion is evaluated once per patient. Its focus is a Bundle of that patient's record (the Patient compartment), and it has these variables: %subject (the Patient), %periodStart and %periodEnd (the period, as dateTimes) and %"Measurement Period". A criterion gives true or false; a single value that exists counts as true.

{
  "code": { "coding": [{ "system": "http://terminology.hl7.org/CodeSystem/measure-population", "code": "numerator" }] },
  "criteria": {
    "language": "text/fhirpath",
    "expression": "entry.resource.ofType(Observation).where(code.coding.where(system = 'http://loinc.org' and code = '4548-4').exists() and effective.ofType(dateTime) >= %periodStart and effective.ofType(dateTime) <= %periodEnd).exists()"
  }
}

The populations nest as the Measure specification says. A patient is in the denominator only if in the initial population. A denominator exclusion takes the patient out before the numerator is judged. A denominator exception counts only for a patient not in the numerator. A proportion's score is (numerator − numerator exclusions) / (denominator − exclusions − exceptions); a ratio's is numerator / denominator; a cohort has counts and no score. Single-criterion stratifiers split every population by their value (sex, say).

Perfuse has no CQL engine. The published CMS eCQMs and HEDIS digital measures are written in CQL, so they cannot be run as they are. A Measure whose criteria are CQL is refused with that reason. Write the logic as FHIRPath instead. Continuous-variable scoring, multi-component stratifiers and Group or Practitioner subjects are refused the same way.

Monitor › Quality measures evaluates a measure over this server's FHIR store or over pasted resources, for a period. It offers three reports: a summary, a summary with the patients in each population, or one patient. The screen shows the populations, the score and the strata, and downloads the MeasureReport. It starts from three examples: blood pressure under control, diabetes with an HbA1c test, and patients seen in the period. They are illustrative, written for this manual, and are not CMS eCQMs or HEDIS measures. Save to the FHIR store checks the Measure and stores it as a Measure resource.

The FHIR endpoint answers Measure/[id]/$evaluate-measure and Measure/$evaluate-measure?measure=<url>, by GET or by POST of a Parameters resource. Parameters:

  • periodStart and periodEnd: required. A date means the whole day.
  • reportType: summary, subject or subject-list.
  • subject: Patient/<id>.
  • reporter: Organization/<id>. When it is absent, the report contains an Organization named by serve -measure-reporter.
  • measure: a Measure sent inline, as a POST parameter.

The report is returned, not stored. The operation is a read, so a SMART token needs read access to Measure and to Patient. A token bound to one patient gets an individual report about that patient and no other.

perfuse measure -measure m.json -period-start 2026-01-01 -period-end 2026-12-31 [-type summary|subject|subject-list] [-subject Patient/ID] [-o out] files... evaluates a measure over FHIR files (a bulk export's NDJSON, Bundles, arrays) without a server. -measure example:example-bp-control runs a shipped example, and -examples lists them.

Verified. The HL7 FHIR validator checks the shipped Measures against FHIR R4, with no errors. It checks a summary, an individual and a subject-list MeasureReport against the DEQM 5.0.0 summary, individual and subject-list profiles, also with no errors. Not verified: equivalence with any published eCQM, since none can be run here without CQL.

8.12 The patient index (MPI)

A master patient index links the records every system keeps for a person (the hospital's MRN, the clinic's, the lab's) into one enterprise identifier, the EUID. It is off until somebody decides to keep one, because it is a table of patients' names, birth dates and identifiers: turn on Settings → Data → Patient index (or serve -mpi) and restart. It lives in mpi.db beside the main database.

Feeding it. Add a destination of type mpi (The patient index in the builder) to the channels that carry registrations. It reads the PID of each HL7 v2 message, or a FHIR Patient on a raw channel. The record's own identifier is the PID-3 repetition of type MR (id_type changes it); the others, and PID-19, become its other identifiers. An assigning authority with a universal identifier (PID-3.4.2 with type ISO) becomes urn:oid:...; one with only a namespace is mapped by authorities, or becomes urn:perfuse:aa:<namespace>. An A40, A34, A18 or A39 merges the person holding MRG-1's identifier into the person holding PID-3's (no_merge ignores them).

destinations:
  - name: index
    type: mpi
    mpi:
      authorities: { HOSP: "urn:oid:1.2.840.114350.1.13.1" }

Matching. A new record is linked in this order:

  1. Trusted identifiers. A record sharing an identifier from a system listed as

trusted (a national identifier, an enterprise number) is the same person, with no scoring. Never list a system a family shares, such as a payer's subscriber number.

  1. Score. Otherwise the people whose records could match are found by blocking

(the same-sounding family name and birth year, the same birth date, or the same phone number) and scored with Fellegi-Sunter weights over family and given name (Jaro-Winkler and Soundex, accents and punctuation ignored, swapped names noticed), birth date (a day and month swapped is partial agreement), sex, postal code, phone and address. Only fields both records have count. The score is the evidence found as a share of the evidence possible. Blocking uses five keys, so one error does not hide a person: family-name sound and birth year, birth date, phone, both names' sounds (in either order), and postal code with the given name's sound.

  1. Decision. By default nothing links on a score alone. A record scoring at

or above the review threshold (60%) becomes a new person and the possible match waits on the review queue; those at or above the automatic threshold (90%) with family name and birth date both agreeing are marked *certain*. Linking two people into one (an overlay) puts one patient's results in another's chart, so automatic linking is a switch an administrator turns on (Link certain matches without asking anybody) once the queue has shown the certain ones are right. With it on, a certain match links when no second person scores as high. Below the review threshold the record is a new person.

National numbers. The NHS number (and Scotland's CHI and Northern Ireland's Health and Care number), Ireland's PPSN and IHI, Germany's KVNR, Austria's SVNR, Switzerland's AHV number, the Netherlands' BSN, Belgium's national register number, Luxembourg's matricule, France's INS (NIR and NIA), Italy's codice fiscale, Spain's DNI/NIE and CIP-SNS, Portugal's SNS number, the Nordic personal numbers (Sweden, Denmark, Norway with its D number, Finland, Iceland), the Baltic codes, Poland's PESEL, the Czech and Slovak birth numbers, and the Hungarian, Romanian, Bulgarian, Croatian, Slovenian and Greek numbers are known to the index. Each is stored one way: under its scheme's system, spaces and dots removed, so the NHS number sent as urn:oid:2.16.840.1.113883.2.1.4.1|999 000 0018 by one system and as https://fhir.nhs.uk/Id/nhs-number|9990000018 by another is one identifier. An HL7 v2 PID-3 repetition whose identifier type is NN and a country's three-letter code (NNDEU, NNNLD) is read as that country's main number, and type NH from the NHS authority as the NHS number. Every number's check digit is tested: one that fails is kept on its record, reported (masked) in the answer, and never used to link or to find anybody, since a mistyped digit usually makes somebody else's number. Denmark's CPR is checked for its date only, because numbers issued since 2007 need not pass the old modulus 11.

The settings list every scheme with its format, its check and the system it is stored under. Systems taken from the country's own FHIR guide (UK Core, fhir.de, Nictiz, CH Core, ANS, HL7 Italia, MedCom, the Swedish, Norwegian and Finnish OID registers) are marked as such; the others are defaults to change to the national value, which a site can do per scheme (the old one is still recognised). Trusting a scheme is one tick. National numbers are shown in full to editors and above by default and masked (the last few characters) to everyone else; Who sees numbers in full changes that, down to viewers or up to administrators only. Where a country's law limits a number's use (France's Référentiel INS, Ireland's PPSN, Belgium's national register number, Germany's §290 SGB V), the settings say so. GET /api/mpi/schemes lists the schemes and POST /api/mpi/check checks one number without storing it. FHIR $match and PIXm answer authorised clients with numbers in full.

A date written day- or month-first (08/15/1972) is kept as written and does not match a year-first one: the US and European orders disagree, and guessing would link the wrong people.

The Patient index screen (Build) shows the counts, finds people by name, number, EUID or birth date, and opens each person: their records, every identifier, and the history of what happened to them and who did it. Editors decide reviews (The same person links them; Different people keeps them apart for good), move a record to another person, take a record out (Not this person), and merge two people. Administrators set the thresholds, the trusted identifier systems, the national numbers and the EUID's system. Every search and every person read is written to the audit log.

Answering others. POST /fhir/Patient/$match (FHIR's operation, as IHE PDQm ITI-119 uses it) takes a Patient and returns the people who could be it, best first, each a Patient whose id is the EUID, with every identifier, search.score, and the match-grade extension (certain, probable, possible); onlyCertainMatches and count are honoured. PIXm (/mhd/Patient/$ihe-pix, ITI-83) answers from the index: every identifier of every record linked to the person, and the EUID.

The API is /api/mpi (summary and search), /api/mpi/persons/{euid}, /api/mpi/find, /api/mpi/reviews, /api/mpi/records (register, link, unlink), /api/mpi/merge and /api/mpi/settings.

Weights. The default Fellegi-Sunter weights come from the record-linkage literature for hospital registration data. Estimate from our records (in the settings, administrators; POST /api/mpi/estimate) fits them to the site's own index with expectation-maximisation, no reviewed pairs needed: m (agreement among true matches) from the pairs blocking brings together, u (agreement by chance) from 20,000 pairs drawn at random, since blocked pairs agree on names and dates by construction. The estimate is shown beside the weights in use and changes nothing until it is used and saved. A field compared on too few pairs keeps its default. The fit assumes the fields are independent given the class, which they are not quite, so treat a large change with suspicion.

Measured. On FEBRL dataset 3 (5,000 synthetic records of 2,000 people, the duplicates carrying typing errors, swapped fields, missing values and nicknames; PERFUSE_TEST_FEBRL), registered in file order with a reviewer answering every queued pair correctly:

Linked on their ownPut in front of a reviewer with the right personMissedWrong links
Automatic linking off (the default)02,649 of 3,0003510
On, default weights1,6371,0123510
On, weights estimated from the records1,6221,0703080

No record was ever linked to the wrong person, and none of the 2,000 new people was queued needlessly. The missed ones differ on nearly every field (a birth date and an address both mistyped, say) and score below the review threshold. FEBRL is synthetic and has few namesakes; a real index has more, so its review queue will be longer, and these numbers are a floor on care, not a promise.

8.13 Delimited

CSV and its relatives. The options are on the source in the builder, and the format reference lists the file keys.

Paths address columns. A named column is available when a header row is declared or the names are given; otherwise columns are positional.

8.13.1 Naming the columns

There are two ways, and they are exclusive. Either the first row of the file names them — the first row names the columns — or you list them yourself, in order. Declaring a header means the row is not treated as data.

If neither is set, columns can only be addressed by position, so a filter written against a name has nothing to match. Turning the header on removes the list of names rather than keeping both: with a header the names come from the file, so a list left behind would be ignored, and a channel that reads as though it chose the names when the file chose them is harder to debug than one that refuses.

8.13.2 What a filter or a script sees

One delimited document becomes many messages, so this needs stating before you write either.

With splitting on, everything runs per row. Declarative steps, the filter and the transformer script all see one row, and a path means *this* row's column. A filter that rejects a row rejects that row and no other, and the received count is rows rather than documents.

With splitting off, the message is the whole document and a write that crosses rows is refused rather than applied to the first one. That refusal is deliberate: silently writing to row one is the behaviour that produces a file which looks transformed and is not.

8.13.3 The three that change what counts as an error

Accept rows with the wrong number of columns is off by default. A row with too many or too few columns is then an error, which is what catches a file whose shape changed without warning. Turning it on accepts the row and leaves the missing columns empty.

Trim spaces around each value fixes the common case of a value padded to a column width. Leave it off where trailing spaces are significant, which they occasionally are in an identifier.

Keep blank lines as rows is off by default and usually should be. On, a blank line becomes a row of empty columns, which matters only when a count has to match the file exactly.

8.14 Raw

No parsing at all. The message is bytes.

Use this when the content is not something Perfuse understands and does not need to: a PDF being moved between systems, a proprietary format, an already-formed document being forwarded unchanged.

The cost is that nothing which depends on structure works — no field-level transformation, no filter on a field, no field-level comparison in the parity checker. A raw channel is a pipe, and it is honest about being one.

8.15 Choosing

If the message is HL7 v2, use hl7, even if you only intend to forward it unchanged. Parsing gives you the trace, the field-level statistics, the ability to filter later and the ability to compare. raw on parseable content buys nothing and loses all of that.

Use raw when parsing would fail or would be a lie about the content.

8.16 SMART Health Links and Cards

CMS's *Kill the Clipboard* asks providers to accept a patient's records from a QR code at check-in, and to hand the visit record back the same way. The QR code holds a SMART Health Link (shlink:/...): an address, a 256-bit key and, optionally, a label, an expiry and a passcode flag. Exchange → Health links reads one.

Receiving. Paste the link, or scan it with the camera where the browser can (Chrome and Edge). Perfuse fetches the manifest, decrypts each file with the key from the link, summarises any FHIR bundle - who the patient is, how many of each resource - and checks any SMART Health Card inside against its issuer's published key. *Deliver to the channel* hands the FHIR content to a channel as a message, which is how it reaches the EHR. The fetch is held to the egress policy at connect time, plain HTTP is refused, and the response of a server that is not a manifest server is never shown, because the address came from a stranger's QR code.

Sharing. *Share* encrypts a FHIR bundle, hosts it, and shows the link and its QR code once. The server keeps the ciphertext and not the key - it cannot open what it hosts, and cannot show the link again. A passcode is hashed; ten wrong ones disable the link. Links expire after 30 days by default, can be revoked, and list how often and by whom they were fetched. Start the server with -public-url set to the https address patients' phones reach; the manifest URL it builds must fit the specification's 128 characters.

Verifying a card. *Verify a card* takes a numeric shc:/ QR (one chunk or several), a .smart-health-card file or a JWS, and says whether the signature matches the issuer's key.

8.17 UN/EDIFACT

dataType: edifact reads UN/EDIFACT interchanges (ISO 9735): ORDERS, INVOIC, DESADV, IFTSTA and the rest, including the European healthcare claim and laboratory messages. The service string advice (UNA) is honoured, with the release character and, for syntax version 4, the repetition separator; functional groups (UNG/UNE) are optional. The interchange is delivered exactly as the sender wrote it.

name: supplier-orders
dataType: edifact
edifact:
  envelope: require      # require (default), warn, or ignore
  split: false           # true: one interchange per message
  acknowledge: contrl    # none (default) or contrl
filter: 'UNB-2.1 == "5412345000013"'
source:
  type: as2              # or http, sftp, file, email ...
  # ...
destinations:
  - name: orders
    type: file
    dir: /data/orders
    filter: 'UNH-2.1 == "ORDERS"'

The envelope. UNT's segment count and reference, UNE's message count and reference, and UNZ's count and reference are compared with what the interchange holds. With require, the default, a mismatch refuses the interchange (it is how a transfer cut short shows); warn accepts it and logs a warning; ignore does not check.

Paths, for the channel and destination filters, count elements and components from 1 in the order the segment is written, as implementation guides number them: BGM-2 (the document number), BGM-1.1, NAD(2)-2.1 (the second NAD's party), LIN-3(2).1 (a repetition), UNB-2.1 (the sender), UNH-2.1 (the message type). A path without an occurrence addresses every one.

Splitting delivers one interchange per message, each with the original UNB (and group header) and trailers that count one, so an order can be filtered, searched and retried on its own. Destination filters see each piece. A file destination names files by message type (ORDERS_INVOIC-20261017.edi), and ${control_id} is the interchange reference.

CONTRL. With acknowledge: contrl, a sender posting over HTTP is answered with a CONTRL syntax and service report: UCI action 7 when the interchange was read and delivered, 4 when it was refused or could not be delivered (HTTP 400 or 502 then). It cannot be combined with split, since a CONTRL answers a whole interchange. Over AS2 the partner's receipt is the acknowledgement.

Not offered: the message directories (which segments ORDERS D.96A allows), so the structure is checked, not the content; transformations, which would rewrite a partner's document; and filter or transformer scripts (a preprocessor runs, before parsing). Checked against pydifact 0.1.8, an independent parser, in both directions.

9 Acknowledgements

An acknowledgement tells the sender what happened. For HL7 v2 over MLLP it is an ACK message; the sender will not send the next message until it arrives, and will usually resend if it does not.

Getting acknowledgements wrong is the most common way an integration silently loses data, because a sender that is being acknowledged has no reason to complain.

9.1 Timing

Covered in message flow, and the summary is: ack.when is on_delivery by default, meaning the acknowledgement is sent only after every enabled destination accepted the message. on_receipt sends it as soon as the message is parsed and queued.

9.2 What Perfuse sends

An AA when the message was accepted, an AE when it was rejected for a reason the sender could act on, and an AR when it was rejected for a reason the sender cannot.

The distinction between AE and AR is who has to do something. AE means the message had a problem — a field that would not parse as a date, a required segment missing — and the sender can correct it and resend. AR means Perfuse could not accept it regardless of content, which usually means a configuration or availability problem at this end. A sender that resends after an AR will get the same answer.

9.3 Identifying this engine

ack.application and ack.facility populate MSH-3 and MSH-4 of the acknowledgement.

When both are empty the acknowledgement mirrors the original receiver — it uses whatever the incoming message had in MSH-5 and MSH-6. That is what most senders expect, and it is the default for that reason.

Set them explicitly when the sender validates them, or when several Perfuse instances handle the same feed and you need to know which one answered.

9.4 The trigger event

ack.include_trigger_event sends ACK^A01 rather than a bare ACK.

Some receivers require it and others reject it, which is why it is an explicit option with no clever default. If a sender is refusing your acknowledgements and the content looks right, this is the first thing to try.

9.5 Negative acknowledgements are not failures to hide

A NACK treated as a success is one of the most damaging misconfigurations possible, and it is common enough to be worth naming. It happens when a destination sends back a negative acknowledgement and the sending side records the *transmission* as successful because the bytes went out and something came back.

Perfuse reads the acknowledgement it receives from an MLLP destination and records the outcome accordingly. An AE or AR from a downstream system is a delivery failure, is retried according to the destination's policy, and counts towards the failure rate that alerts fire on.

If you are migrating from another engine, this is worth verifying explicitly on your own traffic rather than assuming. The parity checker will show it: a destination that the old engine recorded as delivered and Perfuse records as failed usually means the old engine was ignoring a NACK.

9.6 Timeouts

Waiting for an acknowledgement is bounded. When it does not arrive in time the delivery is a failure and is queued for retry.

A timeout is not the same as a rejection and is recorded differently. A rejection means the far side considered the message and declined it; a timeout means you do not know whether it was processed. That distinction decides whether a retry is safe: retrying after a rejection is pointless, and retrying after a timeout risks a duplicate. Perfuse retries after a timeout, because a missing clinical message is generally worse than a duplicate one, and the receiving system is generally able to detect a repeated message control ID.

Whether that trade is right for a given feed is a judgement, and it is worth making deliberately for anything where a duplicate has a clinical consequence — an order, a medication administration — rather than accepting the default silently.

9.7 Sources that do not acknowledge

Not every transport has the concept. A file source has nowhere to send an acknowledgement; a broker source acknowledges to the broker rather than to the original sender; an HTTP source answers with a status code.

Where the source cannot deliver the acknowledgement mode a channel asks for, the channel is refused at load rather than starting and silently not acknowledging. A channel that believes it is acknowledging and is not is worse than one that does not start.

9.8 X12 acknowledgements

Everything above is about HL7 v2. X12 has its own acknowledgements, and unlike v2 they are off by default: an empty setting means none.

That default is the safe one, because a trading partner who is not expecting an acknowledgement and receives one may treat it as an unsolicited interchange. The acknowledge key on an X12 channel takes one of four values:

ValueWhat is sent
noneNothing. The default.
999Implementation acknowledgement. Supersedes the 997 for HIPAA transactions.
997Functional acknowledgement. Older, and still what many payer connections expect.
ta1Interchange acknowledgement, about the envelope rather than its contents.

Which one to send is a property of the trading partner relationship rather than of the message, which is why it has to be configured and cannot be inferred. A 999 supersedes a 997 for HIPAA transactions, but plenty of partners — older payer connections especially — are set up to expect a 997 and will treat a 999 as an unrecognised file.

Sending the wrong acknowledgement is worse than sending none. It answers a question nobody asked while leaving the real one open, and the sender has no way to tell the difference.

9.9 HL7 v3 acknowledgements

An HL7 v3 channel sends an acknowledgement by default, which is the opposite of X12 and worth knowing.

The reason is the transport rather than the standard: a v3 interaction generally arrives over a synchronous connection with the sending application waiting on a reply, and a sender that receives nothing will usually retry. That is how a patient gets registered three times.

Turning it off is possible and has to be done explicitly — "not set" and "set to false" are deliberately different, so the default stands unless somebody said otherwise.

10 Delivery, Queueing and Retry

A destination that cannot be reached is the normal case, not the exception. Systems restart, networks drop, and a hospital interface engine is unavailable during its own maintenance window. Delivery is built around that.

10.1 Queue per destination

Each destination has its own queue. A destination that is failing does not affect the others, and each has its own depth, age and retry state.

Queueing is per destination rather than per channel because the failure is per destination. A channel-wide queue would hold up delivery to three working systems because a fourth is down, and the two situations — one destination unavailable, all four unavailable — would look the same in the statistics.

10.2 Queue settings

queue on a destination:

KeyWhat it does
enabledWhether failed messages are queued at all.
max_attemptsHow many times to try before giving up.
backoffWait before the first retry.
max_backoffCeiling on the growing wait.
max_depthMost messages to hold. Unset means no limit.
retain_hoursHow long a delivered message is kept. Unset keeps it indefinitely.

The last two were missing from this table and from the builder, and they are the pair that stops a disk filling. A queue with neither grows until the filesystem is full, and that failure arrives as something unrelated breaking — a write failing somewhere else entirely — rather than as a queue problem.

Backoff grows between attempts up to max_backoff. Growing rather than fixed, because a destination that is down is usually down for minutes rather than seconds, and retrying every second for ten minutes produces several hundred log entries that bury the one that matters.

10.3 Ordering is preserved

Within a destination, messages are delivered in arrival order, and a message that fails holds its place. Later messages wait behind it rather than overtaking.

This is the right default for clinical data — an admission and a discharge applied in the wrong order is worse than both being late — and it has a consequence worth understanding: one message that always fails will hold up everything behind it.

That is why queue depth and queue age are separate alert conditions. A deep queue that is draining is a busy system. A queue whose oldest message keeps getting older is stuck, and the alert kinds queue-depth, queue-age and queue-stuck distinguish them.

A poison message — one that will never succeed no matter how often it is retried — is the specific failure this design is vulnerable to. max_attempts is what limits the damage: after it, the message is recorded as failed and the queue moves on. Setting max_attempts very high in the hope of eventual success converts a single failed message into a stopped feed.

10.4 When queueing is disabled

With queue.enabled false, a failed delivery is a failed delivery. It is recorded and not retried.
