  6. Run Perfuse in shadow alongside Mirth on live traffic, delivering nowhere, and compare again over a period that includes a weekend and a month end.
  7. Cut over one channel, not all of them.

15.6 What only runs on one vendor's software

The migration screen answers a second question, and it is worth asking before you have any opinion about Perfuse: how much of your integration logic can only run on the software you have.

Every channel gets a Portability verdict, from the same scan that produces the migration notes. It runs against a channel export, so it needs nothing installed and changes nothing.

15.6.1 The distinction is the whole point

Most Java in Mirth scripts is not lock-in. SimpleDateFormat, HashMap, Apache Commons — all of it working around a JavaScript engine from 2009, all of it ordinary, all of it with direct equivalents. A channel with three hundred of those references is fully portable, and a tool that reported "300 Java references, you are trapped" would be lying to you.

Lock-in is specifically the calls that need the vendor's own server: com.mirth.connect.* and vendor jars. Five of those and you are stuck. Five hundred of the other kind and you are free.

So the verdict leads with the number that matters and says in the same sentence that the larger number is not it. Where there are vendor calls, they are listed by name — a count is an assertion, and a list is something your own engineer can go and check.

A third category is kept separate: capability that exists but not from a script. Starting a channel from JavaScript has no equivalent here, and there is an API endpoint that does exactly the same thing. Calling that lock-in would overstate the finding.

15.6.2 Java calls that run unchanged

The commonest of those Java classes run as they are written. Perfuse has no Java runtime, so these are JavaScript classes with the same names and methods, enough for the way channel scripts use them; importPackage(java.util) and importClass(...) bring them into scope by their short names, as in Rhino (JavaScript's own Date, Map, Set, Math, String and Boolean are never replaced: the Java ones are still there by full name). The migration report counts each of these calls as already supported.

PackageClasses
java.utilArrayList, LinkedList, Vector, List, Collection, HashMap, LinkedHashMap, Hashtable, TreeMap, Properties, HashSet, LinkedHashSet, TreeSet, Arrays, Collections, Date, Calendar, GregorianCalendar, UUID, Base64, regex.Pattern (with Matcher), concurrent.ConcurrentHashMap
java.langString, StringBuilder, StringBuffer, Integer, Long, Short, Byte, Double, Float, Boolean, Math, System (time and line separator only)
java.text, java.math, java.net, java.nioSimpleDateFormat and DateFormat (Mirth's pattern letters), BigDecimal (as a double), URLEncoder, URLDecoder, StandardCharsets
Apache Commonslang3.StringUtils and lang.StringUtils (about sixty methods), StringEscapeUtils, codec.binary.Base64

They behave as Java does where scripts depend on it: Integer.parseInt("x") throws NumberFormatException, String.replaceAll and split take regular expressions while replace is literal, maps key strings by value, TreeMap and TreeSet iterate in order, and SimpleDateFormat.parse returns a date getTime() works on. A method a script calls that the equivalent lacks fails at that line, naming it; any other Java class fails by name, as before. BigDecimal is a double here, so it does not keep Java's exact decimal arithmetic: check scripts that use it for money.

15.6.3 External scripts are reported as unknown

A step that runs an external script file references a path on the old server, and the file is not in the export. Nothing can be said about what it contains, so it is counted as unknown rather than assumed either way. Fetch those files before concluding anything — in both directions.

15.6.4 Perfuse's own lock-in, stated in the same breath

This section would be dishonest without it.

Perfuse is Apache 2.0, which is irrevocable for code already released. You can fork what exists today and nobody can withdraw that. Channels are YAML files and scripts are ordinary JavaScript, so there is no proprietary surface to call — Mirth's flavour of Java dependency is structurally unavailable here, which is the one place a limitation is genuinely a feature.

What that does not promise: that a future version stays open, that a hosted service exists at a price you like, or that migrating away from Perfuse costs nothing. Moving any integration layer means re-testing every channel against real traffic. What you are choosing is whether the artefacts you build are readable and portable, and whether the exit depends on a licence somebody else controls.

15.6.5 Setting up step 6

The Shadow section starts a comparison. Choose the channel that is running, choose the candidate to compare against from the list, and start.

Three things about it are worth knowing before you rely on the numbers.

The candidate is chosen from a list, not typed. A shadow names a channel file, and a name that does not resolve makes the *live* channel invalid — Perfuse refuses a channel wholesale when anything it references is broken. So a typo does not produce a warning, it makes the running channel disappear.

The share is a percentage. Lower it only on a busy feed, and know what it costs: sampling reduces work and confidence in the same proportion, and the message that would have shown the difference is the unusual one.

Fields to ignore is usually needed. A channel that stamps a timestamp or a sequence number differs on every single message, which reports a difference rate of 100% and tells you nothing. MSH-7 and MSH-10 are the common pair.

A comparison is written to the channel file as soon as you start it, and begins observing when the channel next loads — the screen says so rather than claiming it is already running. Stopping a comparison leaves the candidate channel in place, because the candidate is the thing meant to go live.

Step 6 is the one that cannot be shortened. The differences that matter are usually in traffic that only appears occasionally — a rare trigger event, a month-end batch, a sender that behaves differently at midnight — and a sample taken over an afternoon will not contain them.

15.7 Going back the other way

A channel can be exported as a Mirth channel file, from the browser: Channels, then To Mirth on the channel you want.

This exists because the objection to replacing a working engine is rarely whether the replacement is any good. It is what happens if the decision turns out to be wrong, and an export that goes back the way it came is the cheapest answer to that.

Read what the dialogue tells you before you take the file. Mirth's format has nowhere to put a Perfuse filter, a transformation chain, a contract or a shadow comparison, so an exported channel does less than the one it came from. The transports and their addresses convert; the logic does not. Each loss is listed by name before the file is offered, and the same list goes into the channel's description so that whoever imports it sees it too.

A channel Mirth cannot express is refused rather than approximated. An S3 destination has no Mirth equivalent, and a channel that imported cleanly while delivering somewhere other than the bucket would be worse than no file at all. The refusal names the destination at fault.

The connector definitions are not written from a reading of Mirth's format. scripts/mirth-dump-connector-templates.sh has Mirth's own serialiser produce the defaults for each connector class, and Perfuse substitutes values into those documents — refusing any setting the template does not already contain. This matters because of how Mirth answers a channel it cannot parse: it does not refuse the import. It stores a channel whose description has been replaced with "This channel is invalid. Verify all required extensions are loaded correctly" and whose destinations have been silently discarded. An exporter checked against its own output rather than against a server passes every test while producing documents that are thrown away, which is exactly what happened here before the exports were tried against a running Mirth.

What crosses today: MLLP and TCP in both directions, HTTP in both directions, file reading and writing, database reading and writing, DICOM in both directions, and SMTP, SOAP and JavaScript destinations. Each pair is verified by importing into a real Mirth 4.5.2 and checking the server did not quietly replace the channel.

16 CMS-0057 for payers

The CMS Interoperability and Prior Authorization final rule (CMS-0057-F) requires impacted payers — Medicare Advantage organisations, state Medicaid and CHIP programs and their managed care plans, and QHP issuers on the federal exchanges — to run four FHIR APIs from 1 January 2027, and to post prior authorisation metrics on their public website every year from 2026.

Perfuse provides the four APIs on its FHIR endpoint and the conversions that fill them: adjudicated claims as CARIN Blue Button, prior authorisations as Da Vinci PDex, and the yearly metrics in the layout of CMS's own template. The CMS-0057 view, under Exchange, says which of the four this instance can answer and what each still needs, and does all three conversions in the browser.

Drugs are out of scope throughout, as they are in the rule's prior authorisation provisions: no pharmacy claims, no formulary, and a metrics row naming a drug is refused rather than counted.

16.1 The four APIs

APIWhat it servesGuidesTurned on by
Patient AccessA member's claims, clinical data and prior authorisations, to an app they chooseCARIN Blue Button 2.2.0, PDex 2.2.0, US Core, SMART App Launch-fhir and a SMART issuer (-smart-issuer)
Provider AccessThe same for a provider's attributed members, in bulkPDex 2.2.0, Da Vinci ATR 2.1.0, Bulk Data-fhir-payer-apis and -fhir-bulk-export
Payer-to-PayerA member's record to another payer, after matching themHRex 1.2.0 $member-match, PDex 2.2.0-fhir-payer-apis
Prior AuthorizationWhat an order needs, the documentation, then the request and decisionDa Vinci CRD 2.2.1, DTR 2.2.0, PAS 2.2.1 with X12 278 translation-fhir, writable, and -crd-rules

The Patient Access API needs no operation of its own: it is the FHIR endpoint's ordinary read and search, protected by SMART, with CARIN and PDex resources in the store. Searching ExplanationOfBenefit supports the parameters CARIN names — patient, identifier, type, service-date, billable-period-start, provider, insurer, coverage — and Coverage supports subscriber-id, identifier and payor.

16.2 Coverage requirements and documentation: CRD and DTR

The Prior Authorization API is in practice three Da Vinci guides together. CRD tells the clinician, while the order is still a draft, whether it is covered, whether it needs prior authorisation and what documentation is wanted. DTR hands the EHR the questionnaire that gathers that documentation. PAS submits the request and returns the decision. Perfuse serves all three as the payer.

-crd-rules rules.yaml serves CRD over CDS Hooks at /cds-services: the order-sign, order-select, order-dispatch and appointment-book hooks, each with a prefetch for the patient's active Coverage. The rules are the payer's policy, kept in a file because they change without software releases:

payer: Springfield Health Plan
rules:
  - description: Home oxygen equipment
    codes: [E0424, E0431]
    covered: covered            # covered, not-covered, conditional
    pa: auth-needed             # no-auth, auth-needed, satisfied, performpa, conditional
    documentation: [clinical]
    questionnaire: https://www.springfield-health-plan.example/fhir/Questionnaire/home-oxygen

Each order gets a systemActions update carrying CRD's coverage-information extension - which the EHR stores on the order and later sends on with the prior authorisation - and a card the clinician reads. An order whose code has no rule is answered conditional, never assumed covered; a request with no Coverage at all gets a card saying so. examples/crd/ has a rules file and a matching questionnaire.

A rule can also carry the rest of what CRD lets a payer say:

  • billing_codes: the codes to bill under.
  • details: limits and costs, such as allowed-period or in-network-copay.
  • contact: who to call.
  • expiry_days: how long the answer holds.
  • depends_on: codes of other orders the answer depends on, so the EHR asks again if they change.
  • doc_purpose: what the documentation is for (withclaim, withorder, retain-doc). withpa is refused, because CRD 2.2.1's

own invariant crd-ci-q4 rejects it.

What the EHR did not prefetch, Perfuse reads from the EHR's fhirServer with the token it sent. If that fails, the answer is indeterminate with CRD's technical reason, never a guess. A Coverage that has ended or is not active is answered not-covered, no-active-coverage.

members: fhir in the rules file resolves the coverage against the payer's own records in this server's FHIR store (start it with -fhir), the same records $member-match reads:

  • a patient who is not a member is answered no-member-found;
  • a member whose coverage does not match is answered coverage-not-found;
  • a prior authorisation already approved there for the service (a ClaimResponse with a preAuthRef) makes the answer satisfied,

with that number.

Discovery declares davinci-crd.version and a coverage-info configuration option. When a client turns the option off, the answer is empty. EHR JWTs are checked as CDS Hooks defines them, with iss, aud, exp, iat and jti and no sub, and a repeated jti is refused.

Calls authenticate with the JWT an EHR signs for each call, from an issuer named in -cds-clients with its JWKS URL - which is how CDS Hooks describes it - or with a Perfuse API token. Discovery is open, as CDS Hooks requires.

DTR: load the questionnaires into the FHIR endpoint, and POST /fhir/Questionnaire/$questionnaire-package returns them as DTR package bundles, with the CQL Libraries they name. The request can name the questionnaire, or send the order and let its coverage-information say which.

Trying it: Payers → Claims & auth → Coverage requirements (CRD) sends an order to this server's rules, or to a payer's CDS service URL, and shows the cards and the coverage information.

16.3 Claims as CARIN Blue Button

perfuse cms0057 carinbb pairs each claim in an 837 with its payment in an 835, by the patient account number the 835 is required to echo back, and writes a FHIR transaction for each: the ExplanationOfBenefit with the Patient, Coverage, Organizations and Practitioners it references.

perfuse cms0057 carinbb -system https://fhir.your-plan.org/identifier -network innetwork \
    claims.837 remittance.835

The profile follows the claim: professional claims (837P) become C4BB-ExplanationOfBenefit-Professional-NonClinician, and institutional ones (837I) inpatient or outpatient by the type of bill. Every entry is a PUT with an id taken from the source identifiers, so loading the same claim twice updates it rather than making a second copy.

Three things are not in either X12 file, and each is handled by saying so rather than by guessing:

  • The identifier system. CARIN requires the member identifier to name the payer's namespace. -system is required; there is no default, because a default would give every payer using Perfuse the same namespace.
  • The network status. Neither transaction says whether the billing provider is in network, and it decides what the member pays. Without -network the benefit payment status is other and the conversion notes why.
  • A claim the 835 does not mention. A CARIN ExplanationOfBenefit is an adjudicated claim. Without the 835 there is no payer claim number, no payment and no member liability, so the claim is reported as skipped and not converted.

Diagnoses are written with their decimal point (J02.9, not J029), because that is how the code system publishes them and a terminology server asked about the other spelling says it does not exist. A dependent's member identifier is the subscriber's, which is what the 837 carries, and the notes say so. An NPI whose check digit is wrong is reported before US Core rejects it.

16.4 Prior authorisations as PDex

perfuse cms0057 priorauth converts a Da Vinci PAS ClaimResponse — or the PAS response bundle that carries one — into a PDex prior authorisation ExplanationOfBenefit, which is what all three data APIs serve.

perfuse cms0057 priorauth pas-response.json pas-claim.json

The PAS Claim is optional and nearly always needed. An ExplanationOfBenefit must say which coverage the decision was made under, and only the request carries that; without it the conversion stops and says so. The review action — approved, denied, pended, with its authorisation number and reason — moves from PAS's extension to PDex's, and the X12 service type keeps its code under the URI PDex binds it to.

16.5 The Provider Access and Payer-to-Payer APIs

With -fhir-payer-apis the FHIR endpoint answers three more operations:

  • POST /fhir/Patient/$member-match — HRex member matching. The coverage identifiers narrow to a few candidates, and the family name and date of birth must then agree; a given name is compared by initial, and gender only when both sides state it. More than one surviving candidate is not a match, and the answer is 422 rather than a guess, because a guess discloses a stranger's claims. An active Consent permitting disclosure is required, since the Payer-to-Payer API is opt-in for the member; -fhir-member-match-without-consent turns that off for testing.
  • GET or POST /fhir/Group/{id}/$davinci-data-export — the Da Vinci bulk export of a Group's current members. exportType chooses the audience: hl7.fhir.us.davinci-pdex#provider-download or #provider-delta for Provider Access, #payertopayer for Payer-to-Payer.
  • GET /fhir/Group/{id}/$export — the standard Bulk Data group export, treated as Provider Access.

The exports apply the rule's exclusions on the way out. Claims lose the payment and every cost-sharing and remittance amount, and keep the services, codes, dates and providers; the CARIN profile is removed from meta.profile because the resource no longer meets it. Payer-to-Payer also leaves out denied prior authorisations. Members holding an active PDex Provider Access opt-out Consent are left out of a provider export, and the log records how many. The patient parameter can only narrow to members of the Group; naming anyone else is refused.

Access to a Group's export is decided by the endpoint's authentication. SMART scopes say what kinds of resource a token may read, not which Group, so each provider needs a token that can reach only its own attribution list. An API token can be limited to Groups when it is made — perfuse token create -label riverside-provider-access -fhir-groups riverside-attributed, or the Limit to FHIR Groups field under Users → Machine credentials — and on the FHIR endpoint it can then read those Groups, run $davinci-data-export or $export on them and fetch what the export produced, and nothing else. Another provider's Group answers not-found rather than forbidden, so a token does not learn which other attribution lists exist; every other request, member matching and ordinary search included, is refused. The limit applies to API tokens; a token from a SMART issuer carries no Group limit. When SMART is configured as well, API tokens are refused unless Settings → FHIR → Accept Perfuse API tokens beside SMART tokens is on (see Security, *Who may call the FHIR endpoint*); a provider's Group-limited token needs it.

16.6 A provider directory: Plan-Net

CMS-0057 also asks payers for a provider directory, and the FHIR endpoint serves one to Da Vinci Plan-Net 1.1.0 with no option to turn on: load the Practitioners, PractitionerRoles, Organizations, OrganizationAffiliations, Locations, HealthcareServices, InsurancePlans and Endpoints, and members' apps can search them. Every Plan-Net search parameter is indexed - a role's network and service, a plan's coverage area, an affiliation's participating organization, an organization's partof - along with the includes and reverse chains (_has) the guide's queries use, and the CapabilityStatement lists the Plan-Net profiles.

Inferno's Plan-Net suite passes 199 tests. The 10 it fails are forward chains where the kit takes its search value from the first Organization it has seen rather than one the searched resources refer to; that is reported to the kit (its issue 34), and the server answers those queries correctly when given a value the data holds.

16.7 Prior authorisation metrics

perfuse cms0057 metrics reads a CSV of decisions exported from the utilisation management system and produces the public page CMS requires, in the layout of its *Prior Authorization Metrics Reporting — Overview and Template*.

perfuse cms0057 metrics -year 2025 -org "Springfield Health Plan" -services services.csv \
    decisions.csv > prior-authorization-metrics-2025.html

Columns are found by header: priority, received, decided and decision are required, and request_id, line_of_business, extended, appealed, appeal_decision, service_code and service_description are read when present. A request belongs to the year it was decided in; one still pending is listed as excluded, never counted. Every row that cannot be read is reported with its line number.

The arithmetic follows the template: for standard and expedited requests separately, the share approved and denied, approved and denied within the decision timeframe, approved after the review time was extended, and approved after appeal — out of appeals, not out of requests — with the mean and median time from receipt to decision. A median under a day is written in hours rather than rounded to "0 days", which the template asks for explicitly. Partly approved requests are counted as approved, since the template has no category for them, and the page says how many there were. Metrics are reported per line of business, which is the level CMS requires.

The page is self-contained — no script, no external stylesheet or font — because it is posted on a public website by whoever maintains that, and anything it loads from elsewhere is something else that can break or track a visitor. -format csv and -format json give the same numbers for other uses. Without -services the page carries a notice that the list of items and services requiring prior authorisation is missing, because CMS requires it on the same page.

16.8 Drugs: the formulary, Real-Time Prescription Benefit and electronic prior authorization

CMS-0062-P (proposed 14 April 2026) would bring drugs into prior authorization: impacted payers would support NCPDP SCRIPT electronic prior authorization, the NCPDP Formulary & Benefit standard and NCPDP Real-Time Prescription Benefit from 1 October 2027, decide drug requests within 24 hours (expedited) and 72 hours (standard), give a specific reason for every denial, and report drug metrics from 2028. Medicare Part D already requires SCRIPT ePA. One file, the formulary, answers all of it:

perfuse serve -fhir -pas -crd-rules payer-rules.yaml -formulary formulary.yaml

examples/formulary/formulary.yaml is a sample whose drugs are real RxNorm concepts with NDCs RxNorm lists for them (checked against RxNav). A file names the payer, its plans (each with the formulary it uses, its group IDs and its cost shares by pharmacy benefit and tier) and its formularies. Each drug is an RxNorm SCD or SBD with its RxNorm group (the SCDG or SBDG the Drug Formulary API requires), its NDCs, a tier, and its limits: prior_authorization, step_therapy, quantity_limit (a quantity in a rolling number of days, and a daily maximum), not_covered, alternatives (RxNorm codes, most preferred first), notes, and questionnaire, the canonical URL of the FHIR Questionnaire a prior authorization asks. cost_30_day is the plan's cost of a 30-day supply, which a coinsurance is a percentage of; without it a coinsurance is quoted as a percentage, never as an invented amount. The whole file is checked when it loads, every problem listed at once. A member is found by the file's members list, by group, or through the FHIR store: a Coverage whose subscriberId is the member ID and whose class of type plan is the plan's id. Payers › Drugs shows the formulary, prices a drug and runs a prior authorization end to end.

16.8.1 The Da Vinci US Drug Formulary API

Published at /formulary/fhir as Da Vinci US Drug Formulary 2.1.0: each plan a PayerInsurancePlan, each formulary a Formulary (both InsurancePlan), each drug a FormularyDrug (MedicationKnowledge) and each entry a FormularyItem (Basic). The guide's searches are served (Basic?code=formulary-item&formulary=...&drug-tier=...&pharmacy-benefit-type=...&period=..., subject:MedicationKnowledge.code, InsurancePlan?formulary-coverage=..., coverage-type, MedicationKnowledge?code=...&doseform=...) with _include=Basic:subject, Basic:formulary and InsurancePlan:formulary-coverage. It is public, with no credential, as CMS requires of a payer's formulary: it is served from a store of its own, in memory, that holds the formulary and nothing else, so being open cannot reach a patient's record. It is read-only. An excluded drug is not published, since the API lists what a formulary covers; RTPB and ePA still answer it as not covered. Verified with the HL7 FHIR validator against the guide's 2.1.0 package with terminology checked: every resource generated from the sample has no errors.

16.8.2 Real-Time Prescription Benefit

POST /ncpdp/rtpb takes an RTPBRequest (NCPDP RTPB v13) with an API token of the operator role and answers an RTPBResponse from the formulary: the coverage status, the formulary status, the member's estimated share and the plan's, and the coverage restrictions, with up to five covered alternatives priced the same way. Restriction codes and the status each maps to follow NCPDP's public RTPB Implementation Recommendations: 75 prior authorization required, 608 step therapy, 76 plan limits exceeded, 7X days supply over the plan limit (a drug not available by mail, say), 70 not covered, all *covered with restrictions* (CR) except 70 (NC). A member with an approved prior authorization on file gets CC and AB8, as the Surescripts guide asks, so the prescriber is not sent to ask again. A request that cannot be priced is NotProcessed with an NCPDP reject code: 52 for a member not found, E7 and 19 for a missing quantity or days supply. The pharmacy benefit is read from the request: a fill over 34 days is a three-month supply, and pharmacy type M is mail order.

16.8.3 Electronic prior authorization (NCPDP SCRIPT)

POST /ncpdp/script takes PAInitiationRequest, PARequest, PAAppealRequest and PACancelRequest with an API token of the operator role, and answers each with its response:

RequestAnswer
PAInitiationRequestClosed when none is needed: CC no prior authorization required, FN within the quantity limit, FM not covered or not on the formulary, FQ an approval on file, CD no such member, CG one already in process. Otherwise Open, with a case ID and the question set.
PARequestDecided as a PAS request: Approved with the authorization number and period, Denied with the reason, or Open under review.
PAAppealRequestA denied case decided again, on the reason in PANote (an appeal without one is asked for it).
PACancelRequestApproved while the case is open or under review, and the PAS request is cancelled; Denied once it is decided.

The question set is the drug's FHIR Questionnaire from the FHIR store, the same one DTR fills in: a boolean becomes a yes/no choice, a choice a choice, a number numeric, a date a date, and anything else free text; groups are flattened in order, and enableWhen is not carried, since SCRIPT branches by choice and not by condition. A drug with no questionnaire is asked three free-text questions: the diagnosis, what was tried, and why this drug. A PARequest becomes a Da Vinci PAS request: a Claim for the drug (RxNorm and NDC), the MedicationRequest it asks for, the patient, prescriber, payer and coverage, and the answers as a QuestionnaireResponse, with any attachments as DocumentReferences. It is then decided as every prior authorization on this server is: the rules file, a decision service (-pas-decider-url), or the reviewer queue, where it is marked Drug and held to the drug timeframes. A decision service sees a drug request exactly as it sees one from an EHR's PAS client. We recommend Vaglio, BioDream's source-available (PolyForm Shield) decision engine, which has drug policies with step therapy; a payer's own engine plugs in through the same -pas-decider-url, and none is needed for the rules file and reviewers to decide.

A decision made after the request was answered (a reviewer's, an appeal's) is sent to the prescriber as an unsolicited PAResponse or PAAppealResponse, routed to the channel named by -epa-reply-channel, which delivers it to the network. Without one, or when delivery fails, the case says the prescriber has not been told and the screen sends it again.

Payers › Drugs › Prior authorization sends the messages a prescriber's system would to this server's own endpoint (start, answer the questions, see the decision), lists every case with what the prescriber was told, and shows the drug figures CMS-0062-P proposes: requests approved, denied and pending, appealed and approved or denied on appeal, the mean and median hours from request to decision, and how many were decided late. Medical-benefit drugs requested through PAS (a HCPCS J code with a MedicationRequest, or an item coded in RxNorm or NDC) are counted as drugs in the PAS figures too.

What this rests on, stated plainly. NCPDP's schemas and implementation guides are licensed. The element names and order here follow the public Surescripts ePA and RTPB companion guides and their examples, and NCPDP's public RTPB recommendations. Two elements the public material never shows are written to the SCRIPT pattern used elsewhere and should be checked against a licensed copy before certification: Closed/ReasonCode and Denied/DenialReason. No message has been checked against NCPDP's XSD or a network's certification. The NCPDP Formulary & Benefit file is not produced: its full layout is in NCPDP's licensed guide, and a partial file would be refused by the network that loads it.

16.9 What was checked, and against what

The CARIN and PDex outputs were validated with the official HL7 FHIR validator, loaded with the published packages (hl7.fhir.us.carin-bb#2.2.0, hl7.fhir.us.davinci-pdex#2.2.0): professional, inpatient and outpatient claims and two PAS decisions, with no errors. The PDex inputs were the PAS guide's own examples rather than shapes written here to agree with the converter. The validator found five defects on the first run — among them that CARIN requires the profile version in meta.profile and the claim-type code system version on institutional claims — and each is fixed and covered by a test.

Building this found a defect older than it: the FHIR store dropped every element its internal model did not declare, so an ExplanationOfBenefit lost its items, diagnoses, adjudication and payment on the way in. The store now keeps whatever a resource carries, and a test loads a CARIN claim through the server and reads it back whole.

CRD's output - the updated order, and the CDS Hooks response against CRD's own logical model - and DTR's package output validate in the official HL7 validator against CRD 2.2.1 and DTR 2.2.0 with no errors.

DTR here is the payer's side of 2.2.0. $questionnaire-package takes the questionnaires named, those CRD named on an order, or the coverage assertion id CRD returned (context, while the same process is serving CRD), and returns each as a package bundle with a QuestionnaireResponse to fill in, the Libraries its CQL needs and their dependencies. The value sets its answers use travel in the package when they are loaded (PUT /fhir/ValueSet/{id}), and $expand lists their codes. $log-questionnaire-errors writes what a DTR app reports to the server log.

Adaptive questionnaires: write the whole questionnaire once, with SDC's questionnaireAdaptive extension and enableWhen on the questions that only apply sometimes. The package hands the app an empty shell, and each $next-question call adds the next question that applies to the answers so far, until there is nothing left to ask and the response is marked completed. The order and the branching are the payer's own rules; questions that do not apply are never shown.

Signing in as a client: DTR and PAS clients reach the payer with SMART Backend Services - a signed JWT exchanged for a token at the payer's authorization server. Point Perfuse at that server with -smart-issuer (and -smart-jwks if its address does not resolve from Perfuse), and pass -smart-backend-services so the discovery document says so. The Inferno DTR suite passes this way with Keycloak as the authorization server.

Perfuse can be that authorization server itself: serve -fhir -smart-clients clients.yaml issues Backend Services tokens at /auth/token to the clients listed, each with the public keys it signs its assertions with and the system scopes it may be granted, and the FHIR endpoint accepts them. No separate identity server is needed for a payer's machine-to-machine clients.

With -smart-users users.yaml as well, members and clinicians sign in there to authorize apps, which the Patient Access API needs: the app sends the person to /auth/authorize, they sign in, a clinician chooses the patient, and both see what the app asks for and can untick any of it. A member is only ever given their own record, whatever the app asks for. Each person in the file names the FHIR resource they are, Patient/… or Practitioner/…, and a password hash from perfuse smart hash; the password itself is never written down.

People can sign in at the organisation's own identity provider instead: -smart-oidc oidc.yaml names it (issuer, client id and where the secret is), and the sign-in page offers "Sign in with" it beside the password. Each person is linked by the provider's subject, oidc_subject in the users file, so the file still decides who may authorize apps and which FHIR resource they are; an account linked to nobody is refused. Codes, refresh tokens and revocations are kept in the database, so a restart signs nobody out.

Users → SMART apps in the console lists the registered apps and the people, and adds, edits and removes them. Each save is checked as the files are at start-up, written to them, and used at once. A confidential app saved without a secret is given one, shown once; a password is set there and never shown back. Removing an app, or a person, stops the access tokens already issued to it or granted by them at once, not when they expire, and their refresh tokens with them.

Scopes may be granular, as SMART App Launch 2 and US Core define them: patient/Observation.rs?category=laboratory reads only the Observations that search would find. A grant that is filtered for a type applies its filter to every search and every read of that type; an unfiltered grant for the type, or for *, lifts it. A filter that cannot be read grants nothing rather than everything.

16.10 Deciding prior authorisation requests: PAS

serve -fhir -pas -crd-rules rules.yaml answers Da Vinci PAS 2.2.1 at POST /fhir/Claim/$submit and POST /fhir/Claim/$inquire. The decisions come from the same rules file as CRD's answers, so the order a clinician is told needs authorisation is decided by the rule that said so:

  - description: Physical therapy
    codes: ["PT"]                       # matched on the item's productOrService, then the code of the order it requests
    system: https://codesystem.x12.org/005010/1365
    covered: covered
    pa: auth-needed
    pa_decision: approve                # approve, deny or pend; empty: not-covered is denied, no-auth approved, the rest pended
    details:
      - {code: allowed-quantity, value: "6"}   # asked for more, it is certified for six (A6, with the authorised detail)
  - description: MRI
    codes: ["62"]
    pa_decision: approve
    pa_alternative: {system: "https://codesystem.x12.org/005010/1365", code: "4"}  # approved as an X-ray instead (an added item)
  - description: Surgery
    codes: ["2"]
    pa: auth-needed                     # pended for a reviewer, asking for:
    questionnaire: https://payer.example.org/Questionnaire/surgery   # a DTR questionnaire
    pa_attachments: ["18776-5"]         # and documents, by LOINC attachment code

Each item gets an X12 review action: A1 certified, A3 not certified, A4 pended, A6 modified, C cancelled (certification type 3). A line that names no service is not decided but answered with an error (X12 901 AG, correct and resubmit). The response echoes what a 278 response echoes — item trace numbers, the requested service dates, the encounter's dates, the transmission identifiers with sender and receiver swapped — and gives each line the payer's own administration reference number. A service no rule names is pended, never approved: a decision nobody wrote down is a decision for a person.

Pended requests wait for a reviewer, who answers with POST /fhir/Claim/$decide:

{"resourceType": "Parameters", "parameter": [
  {"name": "claimResponse", "valueString": "<the ClaimResponse id>"},
  {"name": "decision", "valueCode": "approve"},
  {"name": "reason", "valueString": "Reviewed with the plan of care."},
  {"name": "reviewer", "valueString": "<the reviewer's NPI>"}]}

decision may also be modify: certified, but not as asked, with quantity (valueDecimal, the units certified) and/or alternative (valueCoding, the service approved instead). The response then says A6 Modified, with the certified detail or the alternative as an added item, and an authorization number. item (valueInteger, repeatable) limits the decision to some lines. The decision becomes a new version of the response, marked as reviewed by a person, and with -fhir-subscriptions it is delivered on the PAS topic (PASSubscriptionTopic, filtered by org-identifier, the requesting organisation's identifier) as the full PAS Response Bundle, in the same transaction that records it. $inquire returns the current response to every request that provider made about that member; another provider's inquiry finds nothing. $decide needs a write scope; $inquire is a read.

Requests and every version of their responses are kept in pas_requests and pas_responses in the server's database.

16.10.1 The reviewer queue

In the web console, Payers → CMS-0057 → Reviewer queue lists the pended requests soonest due first: an expedited request (Claim.priority stat) is due 72 hours after it arrived, a standard one 7 days. Choosing one shows the member, the provider, each service with the units asked for and where it stands, what the response asked for, and every document received for it, with plain-text documents shown inline. An editor records the decision there: approve, deny, or modify (units certified, or another service approved instead), with a reason and the reviewer's NPI. A modification has to certify fewer units than were asked for; as many or more is an approval, and is refused as a modification. A service approved instead starts in the code system of the one asked for. It is the same decision $decide makes, so the provider's subscription delivers it. Reading a request and deciding it are both written to the audit log.

Monitor → Team dashboards → Prior authorization shows, for the last 30 days, how many standard and expedited requests were decided within CMS-0057's timeframes, decided late, still waiting in time and overdue now, with the median time to decide, and how the services asked for were answered: approved, modified, denied or still pended. These two tiles are read from the requests themselves, so they are not in the dashboard's Grafana export, which says so in its description.

16.10.2 Receiving the documents a pended request asks for

A pended response asks for documents with a Task and one CommunicationRequest per document. The provider sends them with Da Vinci CDex $submit-attachment (POST /fhir/$submit-attachment): the TrackingId is the CommunicationRequest's identifier, the attachment control number (the Task's identifier is accepted too), AttachTo is preauthorization, and MemberId must be the request's member. Each attachment's DocumentReference or QuestionnaireResponse is kept with the request and shown to its reviewer; a document arriving decides nothing.

16.10.3 Deciding in an X12 utilization management system

Most payers decide prior authorizations in a UM system that speaks X12 278. With -pas-278-url, Perfuse is the FHIR front of that system: each $submit is sent as one 278 request (005010X217), every item a patient event carrying its service type, procedure code, diagnoses, date and quantity, and the 278 response's action code and authorization number become the item's review action. Local rules are not consulted.

perfuse serve -fhir -pas -pas-278-url https://um.example/x12/278 -pas-278-receiver ACMEUM \
  -pas-278-token-env UM_TOKEN

A UM system that pends first and decides later posts its later 278 response to POST /fhir/Claim/$decide-278; each event is matched to its request by the trace number Perfuse sent, and decided as $decide would decide it. If the UM system cannot be reached, or answers with something that is not a 278 response, every item is pended for a reviewer and the provider is told only that; the reason is in the server log.

16.10.4 Deciding on the clinical evidence, with a decision service

A rules file decides by service code: a code needs no authorization, is not covered, or goes to a reviewer. Whether this patient meets the criteria for it is a different question, answered from the conditions, questionnaire answers and documents the provider sent with the request. With -pas-decider-url, every item the rules leave to a reviewer is first put to a decision service, such as Vaglio, which reads the same PAS request Bundle through Perfuse's published pas package. A public Vaglio, on invented requests only, runs beside Perfuse's test instance at https://vaglio.perfuse.health:8443 (sign in as demo, password try-vaglio-demo; it can look at everything and change nothing): every decision there has its proof, a plain-language letter and a receipt anyone can check.

perfuse serve -fhir -pas -crd-rules payer-rules.yaml -pas-decider-url https://vaglio.example/api/pas/decide \
  -pas-decider-token-env VAGLIO_TOKEN

The URL must be https, or plain http to this machine (localhost, 127.0.0.1), because it carries a patient's record and the token. Vaglio itself answers on https only; when it uses a certificate from its own authority, pass that authority with -pas-decider-ca DATA/tls/ca.crt (or any payer's internal authority the service's certificate comes from). The service is sent the request Bundle as the provider sent it, POST <url>?item=<sequence> (application/fhir+json), and answers {"decision": "approve" | "deny" | "pend", "why": "...", "requested": ["..."]}, or 404 when it decides nothing for that item, which leaves the rules' answer standing. why is the response's process note and is required for a denial, which is otherwise pended; requested is said to the provider as the information a pended request needs. A pend may also carry "questionnaire": "<canonical>" and "attachments": ["<LOINC>", ...], which are asked for in the response's CDex Task and CommunicationRequests beside whatever DTR questionnaire or attachments the rule asks for (the rule's questionnaire wins, since a Task asks for one; a code that is not LOINC's shape is dropped and logged). A rule that approves or denies is the payer's coverage policy and is not sent to the service. A failure, a timeout or an answer that is not one of the three decisions pends the item for a reviewer, with the reason in the server log: nothing here turns a failure into an approval. It cannot be combined with -pas-278-url, where the UM system answers every item.

Advice before submitting. With -crd-advisor-url beside -crd-rules, CRD also asks a decision service what a prior authorization request for each order needing one would be told as the record stands, and shows the answer as one more card: "Before you submit: a request for this would need: ...", or "would be approved", with what is still needed, the questionnaire that asks for it, and what would approve it. Vaglio answers at /api/pas/advise, with the same token and authority as above:

perfuse serve -fhir -pas -crd-rules payer-rules.yaml \
  -crd-advisor-url https://vaglio.example/api/pas/advise -crd-advisor-token-env VAGLIO_TOKEN

CRD sends POST <url> with {"service": {"system": "...", "code": "..."}, "resources": [ ... ]}, the resources being the hook's prefetch (the patient first, the coverage left out), and reads {"decides": true, "answer": "approve" | "pend" | "deny", "summary": "...", "missing": [...], "questionnaire": "...", "toApprove": [[...]], "policyTitle": "..."}. It is advice and changes nothing: the coverage-information, the other cards and the PAS decision are what the rules say. It waits at most three seconds, and no answer, a failure, or "decides": false leaves the response exactly as it is without it. Perfuse never needs a decision service.

The request carries X-PAS-Request-Id, the id the response is known by. A service with its own reviewers that pends an item can send their decision later as any reviewer's is sent: Claim/$decide with that id as claimResponse, the item's sequence, and the decision and reason. The provider's subscription delivers it as it would one made in the reviewer queue.

A subscription with id-only content tells the provider which answer changed without saying what it is: the notification's focus is Bundle/<id>. GET /fhir/Bundle/<id> returns the latest PAS Response Bundle, GET /fhir/Bundle/<id>/_history/<n> the version a notification sent, and GET /fhir/ClaimResponse/<id> the ClaimResponse alone. A token bound to one patient is told they do not exist, as for any record outside its patient.

Verified with the official Inferno PAS Server v2.2.1 suite (see verification). Not sent, though PAS marks them must-support: ClaimResponse.adjudication with a review action, ClaimResponse.extension:authorizedProvider and ClaimResponse.item.extension:communicatedDiagnosis, because the extensions they need are not allowed there by their own context in PAS 2.2.1 and the validator rejects a response carrying them; and request.extension:DataAbsentReason, because Perfuse always knows the request. Not implemented: forwarding a request to a payer's utilization management system as an X12 278, and attachments sent back with CDex.

A rules file is checked against CRD's own invariants when it loads: a questionnaire needs documentation, a service that is not covered cannot need authorisation, satisfied belongs to one case and not a rule, and anything conditional must give a reason.

Not verified: the exports and member matching against another payer's implementation, the metrics against a payer's published figures, and CRD against a real EHR's CDS Hooks client.

16.11 CMS Aligned Networks

The CMS Interoperability Framework (CMS Health Technology Ecosystem) asks health data networks to self-attest to 26 criteria and be listed as CMS Aligned Networks; EHRs, providers, payers and apps connect to them. Criteria 13 to 16 have been in effect since 4 July 2026: FHIR APIs on US Core with a full CapabilityStatement and Bulk Data, documents as human-readable FHIR attachments, appointment and encounter notifications by FHIR Subscription, and a record locator.

Administer › CMS Aligned Network lists every criterion with what Perfuse provides for it and where this server stands, read from what is switched on here:

StatusMeaning
Ready herePerfuse provides it and it is on.
Ready, with a gapIt is on, and part of the criterion is outside it (named on the screen).
Built, not onPerfuse provides it; the screen names the setting or flag that turns it on.
Yours to doThe organisation's part: an agreement, a certification, publishing to the National Provider Directory.
Not builtSoftware would do it, and Perfuse does not.

What the four in effect need from this server:

CriterionIn PerfuseTurn on
13 FHIR APIs, US Core, Bulk DataThe FHIR endpoint with its CapabilityStatement, and $export-fhir, -fhir-bulk-export
14 Documents as FHIR attachmentsDocumentReference with Binary, and IHE MHD over the XDS registry-fhir (MHD also -xds)
15 Encounter and appointment notificationsTopic-based Subscriptions (R5 Backport for R4) from ADT and SIU feeds-fhir-subscriptions
16 Record locatorThe XDS.b registry, XCA for other communities, PIXm-xds, -xds-home-community

The screen is a worksheet for the attestation, not a certification: CMS reviews networks it suspects do not meet the criteria, and the framework's own text is the authority. Not built today: open quality gap queries (criterion 8), a payer's query for a claim's encounter data (9), reporting query metrics to the National Provider Directory (19), checking providers against that directory (6), reading mobile driver's licences or digital insurance cards (3, 23), and enforcing a general consent policy on every read (5). IAL2 identity proofing is the identity service's; Perfuse accepts the tokens of the one you choose.

17 Security

Perfuse handles clinical data. This chapter is what it does about that and what it leaves to you.

17.1 Transport security

TLS on the web interface is configured with -tls-cert and -tls-key. Without them the interface is plain HTTP, which is acceptable on a loopback address for a first look and nowhere else — it carries credentials and message content, both readable on the wire.

TLS on channel transports is per source and per destination, under a tls block. The available settings are in the source and destination references.

insecure_skip_verify accepts any certificate. It exists because hospital systems routinely present certificates that will not verify, and refusing to connect would mean refusing to integrate. It is named to be conspicuous, and using it means the connection is encrypted but not authenticated — you have protection against passive interception and none against an interposed server.

An unverified TLS connection is not the same as a verified one, and the distinction is worth recording somewhere your auditors will find it. The better fix is almost always to obtain the far end's certificate and trust it explicitly rather than trusting everything.

17.2 Authentication and roles

Users are held in the database. Each has one of five roles, and each role includes everything in the ones above it:

RoleCan
MonitorSee whether things are working: channel status, counts, the flow map, alerts, dashboards, metrics and the queue's depth. No message contents, channel files or queued messages.
ViewerAlso read messages, channel files, traces and the activity log.
OperatorAlso resend: reprocess a message, replay a channel and retry queued deliveries.
EditorAlso create and change channels, and run comparisons.
AdminAlso start and stop channels, clear queues, and manage people, tokens and the server's settings.

A sixth role, Platform, belongs to whoever runs a shared server: it creates tenants and sets their limits, and holds no channels of its own.

Monitor is for a help desk, or for an operations screen on a wall: everything needed to see that a feed has stopped, and nothing that names a patient. Viewer can read message content, which is clinical data; it is a low-privilege role for *configuration*, not for privacy. Operator separates fixing a failed delivery at night from changing what a feed does: the person who resends need not be able to rewrite the channel.

17.2.1 Channel groups

A person (or an API token) can be limited to some channel groups: they then see and act on only the channels whose group is one of theirs. This is the arrangement of a hospital group on one server, each facility's analysts looking after their own feeds and a central team, with no limit, looking after all of them. Rhapsody calls it a locker.

The limit applies everywhere at once, because it is applied to the request rather than remembered by each screen: the channel list, the dashboard and flow map, messages and their search, counts and charts, the queue, alerts, metrics, certificates, contracts and code-set use. A channel outside the person's groups does not exist for them; asking for it by name answers *not found*, so its name is not confirmed either. A channel with no group is seen only by people with no limit.

Saving a channel keeps it inside the person's groups: they cannot create a channel in another group, or move one out of their own. What every facility shares - code tables, script libraries, prior-authorisation decisions - they can read but not change. Server-wide figures (prior authorisation, token use, tunnels) are shown only to people with no limit, and so are metrics and alerts about the whole server rather than a channel.

An administrator limited to groups is a facility's own administrator. They manage the people and tokens of their groups, start and stop their channels and clear their queues. A colleague they add gets their groups unless they name some of them, they cannot give a group they do not have, and they never see or change anybody with no limit. Everything else an administrator can do changes the whole server - settings, sign-on, restarting, alert rules, branding, fleet, the SMART server - and is refused to them.

Groups are set in Users (and for tokens in Tokens), as a comma-separated list. Each change is recorded in the activity log and takes effect at the person's next request.

A different role in each group

A person can hold a different role in each of their groups: lab=editor, adt in Users edits the lab's channels and has the role chosen beside it - say viewer - in the admissions ones. They see both groups' channels on every screen. Saving, starting or resending one in a group where their role is too low is refused with the role they hold there; it does not pretend the channel is missing, since they can see it. Their role at the top of the screen is the strongest they hold, which opens the screens that role uses, and every action on those screens is still narrowed to the groups where they hold it.

Only people limited to channel groups can have one. Somebody who sees every channel has one role, because "editor of the lab's channels" for a person with no limit would be editor of everything that belongs to no group, and of the server-wide pages. A facility's administrator can give a role per group only in the groups they administer, and never above their own role there.

17.3 Who may call the FHIR endpoint

The FHIR endpoint (serve -fhir) checks one of two kinds of token:

  • Perfuse API tokens, made under Users → Machine credentials or with perfuse token create. Each carries a role, and can be

limited to FHIR Groups. This is the default when no SMART authorization server is configured.

  • SMART access tokens, from Perfuse's own authorization server (-smart-clients) or an outside one (-smart-issuer). Each

carries scopes, and the endpoint holds every request to them.

With SMART configured, only SMART tokens are accepted, so every caller is held to its scopes. An API token has a role and no scopes: a viewer token can read every patient's record. A payer who turned SMART on to limit each app to what it was granted should not have a way in that is not limited.

Some sites need both all the same - an internal claims feed that holds an API token and has no SMART client, beside member apps that sign in through SMART. For those, Settings → FHIR → Accept Perfuse API tokens beside SMART tokens (-fhir-api-tokens at start-up) lets API tokens in as well. It is off by default, it takes effect on the next request in either direction, and changing it is written to the audit log. A token is told apart by its shape: a SMART token is a signed JWT, and an API token never is, so one is never checked as the other. Where you can, give such a system a SMART Backend Services client instead, with only the system scopes it needs.

17.3.1 A FHIR endpoint open to strangers

A public test server gives out credentials that can write, because testing prior authorization or subscriptions needs writes. Two limits keep one client from spoiling it for everybody, both under Settings → FHIR and both off by default:

  • Writes a minute from one client (-fhir-writes-per-minute): creates, updates and deletes from one client address. Over it, a

write is answered 429 with Retry-After: 60. Reads and searches, including searches by POST, are never counted.

  • Most resources held (-fhir-max-resources): past it, new resources are refused with 507, while updates and deletes still work.

A hospital's own endpoint needs neither.

17.4 AI agents: the MCP server

With Settings › Security › AI agents (MCP) on (or serve -mcp), Perfuse serves the Model Context Protocol at /mcp, so an AI agent such as Claude, ChatGPT or an IDE assistant can ask it what is happening. It is off by default, and switching it off stops every agent on its next call.

An agent connects with an API token from Administer › Users › Machine credentials as a bearer token, never with a person's browser session (that is refused, so an agent is never acting with a person's full access). A client configuration looks like:

{ "mcpServers": { "perfuse": { "type": "http", "url": "https://perfuse.example/mcp",
    "headers": { "Authorization": "Bearer <token>" } } } }
ToolRoleWhat it does
server_status, list_channels, queue, alertsmonitorWhether things work, with no message contents
get_channel, search_messages, get_message, find_patient, fhir_searchviewerChannels, messages (with contents), a patient's messages across formats, a FHIR search
retry_delivery, reprocess_messageoperator, and mcp:writeRetry a queued delivery, send a message through its channel again
start_channel, stop_channeladmin, and mcp:writeStart or stop a channel

Every tool is answered by the same route or FHIR search a person's screen uses, called with the agent's own token, so the token's role, channel groups and tenant decide what it sees, exactly as they do in the console; and fhir_search gets the token's own access on the FHIR endpoint (which may refuse API tokens altogether; see the FHIR section above). An agent is offered only the tools its token may use. Reading is the default: a tool that changes something also needs the token's mcp:write scope ("Let an AI agent act with it" when issuing the token), so a token issued for a reporting agent cannot be talked into acting by a prompt. A monitor token's answers have message contents removed, whatever a route returns.

Every call is in Activity as mcp.tool with the token's name, the tool, its arguments and the outcome (ok, refused, invalid, rate-limited or the HTTP status); mcp.connect records which client connected. Each token may make 60 tool calls a minute (Tool calls a minute from one token); over that, a call is answered with an error the agent can read, and nothing is done. A request from a browser page on another origin is refused, which MCP asks of a server to stop DNS rebinding. The transport is MCP's Streamable HTTP, answered as JSON with no event stream; protocol versions 2025-06-18, 2025-03-26 and 2024-11-05 are accepted. Messages and FHIR resources hold patient data: an agent's answers go to whatever model it runs on, so give a token to an agent only where that model is allowed to see them, and give it the lowest role that does the job.

17.5 A demonstration account

serve -demo-account demo:PASSWORD creates a shared account for a public demonstration server, and the sign-in page shows its username and password with a button that signs in with them. The account is a viewer, so it can look at everything and change nothing. Unlike any other viewer it may also open the administrators' screens (users, machine tokens, settings, data protection, the fleet's peers), read-only: a demonstration is judged by its screens. It reads them as a viewer reads channels, with every credential shown as set or not, never its value, and every change refused. It cannot change its password or add a passkey, because the first visitor to do either would lock out everyone after them. Each start puts it back as a viewer with the published password. The password must be at least 12 characters, like any other. Only use it on a server holding test data: anyone who reads the sign-in page can read every message on the server.

17.6 Audit

Configuration changes, logins, and access to message content are recorded with who, what and when.

The audit log is in the same database as the messages. That is convenient and it is a limitation: somebody who can modify the database can modify the audit log. If your requirements include tamper-evident audit, the log needs shipping somewhere append-only, and Perfuse's own log output is the way to do that.

17.7 Secrets in configuration

Passwords, passcodes and keys appear in channel configuration, which means they are in the YAML files.

Two consequences. The files need filesystem permissions that reflect what they contain, and if they are in version control — which is otherwise recommended — the repository holds credentials and must be treated accordingly.

Where a value is a secret, the interface renders it as a secret and the API does not return it once saved. That protects it from being read over somebody's shoulder or leaked in a screenshot. It does not protect the file.

17.8 What is in the database

The message store holds messages as received, which for a clinical feed means patient-identifiable data. It should be on encrypted storage, backed up as clinical data with the retention that implies, and access-controlled at the filesystem level as well as through Perfuse's own roles.

cleanup.periodDays limits how long it is kept. See the message store for the tension between that and having enough history for rhythm-aware alerting.

17.9 Data protection (GDPR and UK GDPR)

The Data protection screen (Administer, administrators only) answers a person's requests under the GDPR and the UK GDPR. It decides nothing on the controller's behalf: whether a request must be granted, and whether an exemption applies, is the controller's decision, recorded with the request.

Finding what is held. A person is named by identifiers: system|value (an NHS number, an MRN with its assigning authority) or a number on its own. With a patient index, those widen to every number of every record linked to the same person, so a request quoting one hospital number finds the records filed under the others. See what is held then searches every store that keeps patient data:

StoreFound by
Messagesthe identity index (Settings → Data → Index patient identifiers); without it, messages cannot be found by identifier
The delivery queuereading each queued message's identifiers
The FHIR storethe Patient resources carrying an identifier, and every resource whose indexed references point at one
Prior authorization requeststhe identifiers of the request's patient
The patient indexthe identifier, or the EUID

What it cannot reach it says: traces sent to a collector (-trace-endpoint), the audit log (kept: it is the record of who did what, this request included), and backups.

Requests. A request is recorded with its kind (access, Article 15; portability, Article 20; erasure, Article 17; restriction, Article 18), who it is from as the controller's records refer to them, the identifiers, and a note (how the identity was confirmed, an extension and its reason). It is due one month after receipt (Article 12(3)); an overdue one is shown in red.

  • Access and portability: Download the copy gives a zip: every message as

received with its record, every FHIR resource with every stored version, prior authorization requests and their answers, and the patient index's records, JSON throughout, with a README saying what each part is. Mark answered closes it.

  • Erasure: a second administrator approves it (whoever recorded it cannot).

Erase now deletes everything found: messages whole (record, body, deliveries, identifiers, attachments no other message uses), queued deliveries (they are not sent), the FHIR resources with their history, prior authorization requests with their answers, attachments and tracking numbers, and the person in the patient index with every linked record and its history (one event records that an erasure happened, and by whom).

  • Restriction: recorded as a legal hold, so nothing is erased or expires while

it lasts.

  • Refuse closes a request with its reason, which the person must be told.

A closed request keeps only the last characters of the person's numbers: it stays as the record of an answer without being another copy of them. Every step is in the audit log with the numbers masked.

Legal holds. A hold names a person by identifiers, with its reason and an optional end date. While it is in force an erasure is refused outright, and the message store's retention window spares the person's messages (records and bodies) until the hold is lifted.

Backups and the erasure ledger. A backup made before an erasure still holds what was erased until it expires. Every erasure is written to a ledger: when, by whom, approved by whom, how much went from each store, and the person's identifiers as salted SHA-256 hashes rather than in clear. After restoring a backup, Apply erasures again hashes the identifiers the restored stores hold with each entry's salt and erases every person the ledger names. Short numbers can be found from their hashes by trying every value, so the ledger is itself personal data and is kept with the database's own protection.

Retention. The screen lists how long each kind of record is kept here and what changes it: message records and bodies by their retention windows, the queue until delivered or removed, the FHIR store, prior authorization requests and the patient index until erased (clinical records are not deleted on a schedule), the audit log kept.

Record of processing (Article 30). Record of processing downloads, as Markdown or CSV, what this server knows of it: the controller, the data protection officer and where the data is kept (Settings → Data → Data protection), the retention of each kind of record, and one processing activity per channel with what it receives, from where, to which destinations, and the hosts it sends to. The purposes, lawful bases and categories of data subject are left for the controller to fill in, and say so. A CSV cell beginning with =, +, - or @ is written with a leading apostrophe, so a spreadsheet does not run it.

Where the data is kept. Where the data is kept (Settings → Data → Data protection) states, in words a regulator can check, where the server, its database and its backups run; the Data protection screen and the record show it. A draft data processing agreement for organisations BioDream hosts is in docs/legal/dpa-template.md; it is a draft for a lawyer, not legal advice.

The API is /api/privacy (summary), /api/privacy/find, /api/privacy/requests (and /{id}/approve, /{id}/export, /{id}/erase, /{id}/close), /api/privacy/holds, /api/privacy/reapply and /api/privacy/register.

17.10 Tenants

A tenant is an isolation boundary: channels, messages, users and settings belong to one, and nothing crosses.

This exists for a service provider running feeds for several practices. Do not use it to separate departments within one organisation that need to see each other's traffic: the isolation is real and there is no cross-tenant view. For that, use channel groups (above), which divide the people of one organisation while letting a central team see everything.

17.10.1 Limits

A platform operator sets each tenant's limits in Tenants, so one customer cannot crowd out the rest of a shared server. Leave a limit empty for none.

  • Channels: how many channels the tenant may have. Creating one more is refused, with the count and the limit.
  • Messages a minute: how many messages its channels together may receive in a minute.
  • Storage: how much stored message content it may keep, in megabytes.

None of them loses a message. Over the rate or the storage limit, a received message is refused the way a busy receiver refuses one, before anything is stored or delivered: MLLP gets no acknowledgement (not a rejection, which some senders take as "do not resend"), HTTP gets 429 with Retry-After, SOAP gets 503, DICOM gets a retryable status, and a queue, bucket or file is left where it was. The sender resends, as every interface engine and EHR does. Nothing already stored is deleted to make room. A raised limit takes effect within a minute, and every change of limits is recorded in the activity log.

17.11 Test data

The generated messages described in testing exist partly as a privacy control. Reproducing a problem elsewhere, sending an example to a vendor, or populating a test environment are all things that otherwise get done with real messages.

The generator's output contains no patient data by construction rather than by redaction. Prefer it to hand-stripped real messages, which is a process that can be done incompletely and usually is.

17.12 Single sign-on

Three mechanisms, and local accounts alongside all of them.

OpenID Connect (-oidc), a directory over LDAP (-ldap), and SAML 2.0 (-saml). Each is configured from Administer → Sign-on and written to the file the flag names, so the screen and the file are two views of one thing rather than two places to keep in step.

Local accounts are always available and cannot be turned off. That is deliberate: an on-premises integration engine whose only way in is an identity provider becomes unreachable exactly when the identity provider is unreachable, and a clinical interface is not a good place to learn that. The sign-on screen tells you how many local administrators exist before it encourages you to depend on anything else.

17.12.1 What a group mapping decides

All three map groups from the directory to Perfuse roles, and somebody in groups matching more than one gets the most privileged. That is the only safe direction to resolve it — the alternative is a person losing access they are entitled to because they are also in a lesser group.

Any of the six roles can be granted: monitor, viewer, operator, editor, admin and platform.

A group can also be limited to some channel groups, in the second box of its row (channel_groups in the file). Once any group names one, every sign-in sets the person's channel groups from the groups that granted their role: somebody in a group that edits the lab's channels and in another that views every channel is an editor of the lab's channels and nothing else. The strongest role is never joined to the widest reach, because neither group grants that. A group with an empty box gives every channel. While no group names any, sign-in leaves channel groups as an administrator set them in Users.

When every group a person matches names channel groups, each channel group gets the strongest role among the groups that name it: with Lab-Interfaces (editor, lab) and Clinical-IT (viewer, lab and adt), the person edits the lab's channels and views the admissions ones, as if set by hand. When one of their groups gives every channel, there is no list to hold a role per group, and the rule above applies.

roles:
  editor: [Lab-Interfaces]
  operator: [Night-Desk]
  viewer: [Clinical-IT]
  channel_groups:
    Lab-Interfaces: [lab, pathology]
    Night-Desk: [adt]

That is the OpenID Connect and SAML file. The directory's file maps each group to one role (roles: {Lab-Interfaces: editor}), and channel_groups sits beside roles there.

A mapping that grants nobody anything is refused rather than saved. A configuration that authenticates people and turns all of them away looks identical to a broken product from the outside: the round trip completes and everybody is denied with nothing explaining why.

Creating an account on first sign-in is off by default. With it off, somebody has to exist in Perfuse before they can sign in, so the directory decides who they are and Perfuse decides who is allowed in.

17.12.2 SAML, specifically

The parts worth knowing before you configure it:

  • Only the certificate you configure is trusted. A certificate carried inside a response is never used. An attacker who can send a document can also put their own certificate in it, and a verifier that reads it is one anybody can authenticate to as anybody.
  • A response has to answer a sign-in this server started. Perfuse remembers the request it sent and refuses a response naming anything else, or naming a request it has already answered. Without that check a valid response is a bearer token for whoever holds it: somebody signs in as themselves, keeps the response, and posts it into your browser, and you are then inside their account with the audit log recording their name.
  • Sign-ins started at the identity provider are therefore off by default. Turn on *Accept sign-ins started at the identity provider* only if you need a portal tile, and knowing that it is what makes the above possible.
  • The group attribute has no default and is required. Entra sends groups, Okta sends whatever the application was configured with, ADFS sends a claim URI. A guess would produce a sign-in that works and grants nobody anything. If a sign-in is refused for having no role, the server log names the attributes that did arrive — which is usually the whole answer.
  • The reply URL must match exactly what is registered at the provider. A response says where it was destined and one addressed elsewhere is refused, because accepting it would mean accepting a response meant for a different service.

17.12.3 What each provider sends

The group attribute is where sign-ins go wrong, and the three common providers each name it differently.

ProviderUsually sendsNotes
KeycloakgroupsNeeds a group membership mapper added to the client.
Entra IDa claim URI ending /claims/groupsSends group object ids unless the application is configured to send names. Map the ids, or change the provider.
Oktawhatever the application was configured withCommonly groups, with a filter deciding which are sent.
ADFSa claim URISigns the Response rather than the Assertion, which Perfuse accepts.

A claim URI may be entered by its final segment: typing groups finds http://schemas.microsoft.com/ws/2008/06/identity/claims/groups. A name that merely ends with the word does not match — excluded_groups is not groups.

Group membership arriving as one comma-separated value is treated as a single group name and matches nothing. That is the correct reading of the standard, and splitting on commas would mean a group legitimately containing one silently became two. If that is what your provider sends, change it there.

Test on the sign-on screen checks everything that can be checked without a person: the settings, the certificate, and whether a sign-in request can be built. It says plainly that it cannot prove somebody can sign in, because that needs a real assertion about a real person and the only way to get one is for them to try.

17.13 Accounts provisioned by the identity provider: SCIM

With -scim (Settings → Sign-in → Automatic accounts), an identity provider such as Okta, Microsoft Entra ID or Google Workspace creates, updates and removes Perfuse accounts itself at /scim/v2/Users, so a person who leaves the organisation loses access when their directory account is disabled, not when somebody remembers. The provider authenticates with an API token holding the admin role, made under Users → Machine credentials, sent as a bearer token.

  • Disabling an account ends its sessions at once, and the server checks that none survived before answering. A provider is

never told an account was disabled when it was not, because a provider does not retry what it was told succeeded.

  • A delete disables first, then deletes, so a failure half way leaves an account nobody can sign in with. The audit history of

a removed account is kept.

  • The role comes from the person's groups or roles, matched by name: a group whose name contains *admin*, *editor*, *operator*,

*viewer* (or *read-only*) or *monitor* gives that role, so APP-Perfuse-Editor and Perfuse Operators work as they are named. Anything else gets -scim-default-role, viewer unless set otherwise. Platform can never be granted this way.

  • No passwords. An account made by SCIM signs in through the identity provider; a second password nobody upstream knows about

would survive a disable there.

  • /scim/v2/ServiceProviderConfig says what is supported: create, read, replace, patch and delete of Users, and filters up to 200

results. Bulk operations, sorting and ETags are not.

17.14 What Perfuse does not do

It does not encrypt the database at rest. Use filesystem or volume encryption.

It does not manage its own certificates or renew them. Point it at files and manage those files with whatever you already use.

SAML has been verified against two independent providers, each with a real browser sign-in, a real assertion and a real session: Keycloak 26 and Microsoft Entra. Two matters more than twice as much as one, because the two disagree in a way that turned out to be load-bearing — Keycloak writes its assertion with namespace prefixes and Entra writes the same elements without them, and the one defect this area has had was in prefix handling.

Start by reading your provider's metadata. Settings, then Sign-on, then the SAML tab: give the metadata URL or paste the document, and the sign-on address and signing certificate are filled in for you. Every SAML provider publishes one of these — Okta, Entra, AWS IAM Identity Center, Keycloak, ADFS — and it is the difference between configuring a provider and transcribing one. Doing it by hand means finding the certificate inside the XML, stripping the line breaks out of the base64 and wrapping it in PEM headers, where a single stray space produces a signature error that says nothing about formatting.

What comes back is described rather than dumped: each certificate's subject, expiry and SHA-256 fingerprint, so you can check it against what your provider's own console shows. An expired certificate is called expired rather than left as a date to compare by eye. Nothing is saved by reading — applying it to the form is a separate click, because a document somebody pasted is not yet a decision about what to trust.

A metadata URL is fetched over https only, and the address is checked against the same policy that governs channel destinations, so this cannot be used to make the server read cloud instance credentials. Redirects are not followed, because a permitted address redirecting to a blocked one would walk straight past that check.

If you are configuring Entra, five things will not match what the examples show you. It sends no email claim at all. Its NameID is an opaque identifier rather than an address. It sends no groups claim unless the application is explicitly configured to emit one, so a role mapping written against groups refuses every sign-in. When groups are emitted they are object GUIDs rather than names, unless your tenant synchronises from on-premises Active Directory. And its claim names live under schemas.microsoft.com/identity where most documentation shows schemas.xmlsoap.org. Perfuse handles all five; they are listed because the first four will otherwise look like faults in Perfuse when they are Entra being Entra.
